Cluster Algebras and Scattering Diagrams Part III Cluster Scattering Diagrams Thanks: This is the final manuscript of Part III of the monograph “Cluster Algebras and Scattering Diagrams”, MSJ Mem. 41 (2023) by the author.
[

Abstract
This is a self-contained exposition of several fundamental properties of cluster scattering diagrams introduced and studied by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich. In particular, detailed proofs are presented for the construction, the mutation invariance, and the positivity of theta functions of cluster scattering diagrams. Throughout the text we highlight the fundamental roles of the dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation in cluster scattering diagrams.



[page=1]

]Tomoki Nakanishi

Graduate School of Mathematics, Nagoya University

[depth=2]

0.0
Introduction to Part III

This is a self-contained exposition of several fundamental properties of

cluster scattering diagrams
(CSDs for short)
introduced and studied by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]
.

Scattering diagrams originated
in the study of homological mirror symmetry,
and they are developed
in the works of
Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS06
,
KS08
,
KS14
]
,
Gross-Siebert
[
GS11
]
,
Gross-Pandharipande-Siebert
[
GPS10
]
,
Gross
[
Gro11
]
,
Carl-Pumperla-Siebert
[
CPS
]
, etc.
The connection to cluster algebras was established
by Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]
,
where CSDs were introduced.
Then, with a scattering diagram technique,
several important conjectures on cluster algebras
were proved.

Here is a brief description of the contents.

•

In the first half (Sections 1–3)
we present a construction of CSDs
without assuming any knowledge on scattering diagrams.
The main result here is the existence and the uniqueness of
a CSD for a given seed (Theorem
0.3.12
).

•

In the second half (Sections 4–7)
we present the fundamental properties of CSDs,
which are necessary to prove several conjectures in cluster algebras,
including

–

the mutation invariance (Theorem
0.6.9
),

–

the positivity of theta functions (Theorems
0.5.2
and
0.7.9
).

•

We present detailed proofs for all propositions
in a self-contained way.

The connection between CSDs and cluster patterns are given in Part II
based on the above results.
Thus, this part is regarded as a supplement to Part II.
Also, it may be read independently as an introductory text
to CSDs.

While the most results are taken from
[
GHKK18
,
KS14
]
,
there are several added or modified proofs and new results due to us.
Most notably, we employ the following approach, which adds
novelty to our presentation:

•

We clearly separate the structure group of walls
and its faithful representation (the
principal
x
-representation
)
employed for the construction of CSDs in
[
GHKK18
]
.
In the first half we only use the structure group itself.
This enables us to lift the assumption of the nondegeneracy for the fixed data
in
[
GHKK18
]
,
thereby makes the construction of CSDs more transparent.
In the second half we apply the principal
x
-representation to
the wall elements when needed.

•

Throughout
we highlight the fundamental role of the
dilogarithm elements
and the
pentagon relation

in CSDs, which is implicit in
[
GHKK18
]
.

More recently, the
quantum CSDs

were studied by Davison-Mandel
[
DM21
]
,
where the counterparts of many results presented here were given.

0.1
Underlying algebraic structure

In this section we introduce
some underlying algebraic structure
behind scattering diagrams
we are going to study.

0.1.1
Fixed data and seed

Definition 0.1.1

(Fixed data)
.

A
fixed data

\Gamma
consists of the following:

•

A lattice
N\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{r}
of rank
r
with
a skew-symmetric bilinear form

\displaystyle\{\cdot,\cdot\}:N\times N\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}.

(0.1.1)

•

A sublattice
N^{\circ}\subset N
of finite index (equivalently, of rank
r
) such that

\displaystyle\{N^{\circ},N\}\subset\mathbb{Z}.

(0.1.2)

•

Positive integers
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}

such that
there is a basis
(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
of
N
, where

(\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}e_{r})
is a basis of
N^{\circ}
.

•

M=\mathrm{Hom}(N,\mathbb{Z})
,
M^{\circ}=\mathrm{Hom}(N^{\circ},\mathbb{Z})
.

We say that
\Gamma
is
nondegenerate
(resp.
degenerate
)
if
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is nondegenerate (resp. degenerate).

For a given
N^{\circ}\subset N
of finite index,
the above
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}
always exist; for example, take the
elementary divisors of the embedding
N^{\circ}\hookrightarrow N
.
However, other choices are equally good.

Let
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
and we regard

\displaystyle M\subset M^{\circ}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}.

(0.1.3)

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:N^{\circ}\times M^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the
canonical paring.
We also write its linear extension

N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

by the same symbol.

Definition 0.1.2

(Seed)
.

A
seed

\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
for a fixed data
\Gamma

is a basis of
N
such that

(\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}e_{r})
is a basis of
N^{\circ}
.

For a seed
\mathfrak{s}
for
\Gamma
,
we have a basis
(e_{1}^{*}
, …,
e_{r}^{*})
of
M
that is dual to
(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
.
Let
f_{i}=e^{*}_{i}/\delta_{i}
.
Then,
we also have a basis
(f_{1},\dots,f_{r})
of
M^{\circ}
that is dual to the basis
(\delta_{1}e_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}e_{r})

of
N^{\circ}
.

Let
\Omega=\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}=(\omega_{ij})
be the
r\times r
skew-symmetric rational matrix
defined by
\omega_{ij}=\{e_{i},e_{j}\}
, and
let
\Delta=\Delta_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})
be the diagonal matrix
with diagonal entries
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}
.
Let
B=B_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}=(b_{ij})
be the
r\times r
integer matrix defined by

\displaystyle B=\Delta\Omega,\quad b_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}.

(0.1.4)

Since
\Omega=\Delta^{-1}B
is skew-symmetric,

B
is
skew-symmetrizable

with a (left) skew-symmetrizer
D=\Delta^{-1}

in the sense of
[
FZ02
]
.

Conversely, for any
r\times r
skew-symmetrizable integer matrix
B=(b_{ij})
,
there is a (not unique) decomposition

\displaystyle B=\Delta\Omega,

(0.1.5)

where
\Delta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})

with positive integers
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}

and
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})
is an
r\times r
skew-symmetric rational matrix.
Then, we set

\displaystyle N=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}e_{i},\quad\{e_{i},e_{j}\}=\omega_{ij},\quad N^{\circ}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}\delta_{i}e_{i}.

(0.1.6)

This yields
a (not unique) pair of a fixed data
\Gamma
and a seed
\mathfrak{s}
such that
B

is given by (
0.1.4
).

The seed
\mathfrak{s}
here is identified with the initial seed
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)

for a cluster pattern or a cluster algebra
in the sense of

[
FZ07
]
with

\displaystyle x_{i}=x^{f_{i}},\quad y_{i}=y^{e_{i}},\quad b_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\},

(0.1.7)

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
,
\mathbf{y}=(y_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
, and
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{r}

are
cluster variables (
x
-variables)
,

coefficients (
y
-variables)
,
and an
exchange matrix
in
[
FZ07
]
,
and
x
and
y
are symbols for the formal exponential.
See Part II for more details.

Remark 0.1.3
.

In (
0.1.2
) we took the transpose of the bilinear form
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
in
[
GHKK18
]

to match the convention of exchange matrices in

[
FZ07
]
in the above correspondence.
As a consequence, every expression concerning the bilinear form
is the transpose of the one in
[
GHKK18
]
throughout Part III.

Let us introduce a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}p^{*}:&N&\rightarrow&M^{\circ}\\
&n&\mapsto&\{\cdot,n\}.\end{matrix}

(0.1.8)

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\langle n^{\prime},p^{*}(n)\rangle=\{n^{\prime},n\}\in\mathbb{Z}\quad(n^{\prime}\in N^{\circ},n\in N).

(0.1.9)

Lemma 0.1.4
.

Let
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
,
B=(b_{ij})
be as above.

(a). We have

\displaystyle p^{*}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{ij}f_{i}.

(0.1.10)

Thus, the matrix representation of
p^{*}
with respect to the above bases is given by
B
.

(b).
\Gamma
is nondegenerate if and only if the matrix
B
is nonsingular.

(c). The map
p^{*}
is injective if and only if
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.

Proof.

(a). One can verify it as

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{i}e_{i},p^{*}(e_{j})\rangle=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}=b_{ij}=\biggl\langle\delta_{i}e_{i},\sum_{k=1}^{r}b_{kj}f_{k}\biggr\rangle.

(0.1.11)

The properties (b) and (c) follow from (a).
∎

Throughout the section,
we fix a seed
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
of a given fixed data
\Gamma

unless otherwise mentioned.

Let
N^{+}
be the set of
positive elements
in
N
with respect to
\mathfrak{s}
, namely,

\displaystyle N^{+}=N_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+}:=\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}e_{i}\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ \sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}>0\biggr\}.

(0.1.12)

We say that
n\in N
is
primitive
if
there is no pair
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
and
n^{\prime}\in N

such that
n=jn^{\prime}
.
Let
N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
be the set of all primitive elements
in
N^{+}
.

We have a natural
degree function

\deg:N^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
given by

\displaystyle\deg:\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}e_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}.

(0.1.13)

For each integer
\ell>0
, we set

\displaystyle(N^{+})^{\leq\ell}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N^{+}\mid\deg(n)\leq\ell\},\quad(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}=(N^{+})^{\leq\ell}\cap N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},

(0.1.14)

\displaystyle(N^{+})^{>\ell}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N^{+}\mid\deg(n)>\ell\},\quad(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{>\ell}=(N^{+})^{>\ell}\cap N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},

(0.1.15)

\displaystyle(N^{+})^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N^{+}\mid\deg(n)=\ell\},\quad(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\ell}=(N^{+})^{\ell}\cap N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}.

(0.1.16)

In particular,
(N^{+})^{\leq\ell}
is a finite set.

0.1.2
Structure group
G

Let us fix a field
\mathbbm{k}
of characteristic zero arbitrarily,
e.g.,
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}
.

For a given fixed data
\Gamma
and a seed
\mathfrak{s}
,
we introduce an
N^{+}
-graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
over
\mathbbm{k}

with generators
X_{n}
(
n\in N^{+}
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\bigoplus_{n\in N^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathbbm{k}X_{n},

(0.1.17)

\displaystyle[X_{n},X_{n^{\prime}}]=\{n,n^{\prime}\}X_{n+n^{\prime}}.

(0.1.18)

The Jacobi identity is easily seen by
the following cyclic expression:

\displaystyle[X_{n_{1}},[X_{n_{2}},X_{n_{3}}]]=(\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\{n_{2},n_{3}\}-\{n_{2},n_{3}\}\{n_{3},n_{1}\})X_{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}}.

(0.1.19)

For each integer
\ell>0
,
we define a Lie algebra ideal and a quotient of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell}:=\bigoplus_{n\in(N^{+})^{>\ell}}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}:=\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\in(N^{+})^{\leq\ell}}\mathfrak{g}_{n}\text{\ (as a vector space)}.

(0.1.20)

It is important that

\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
is a
nilpotent
Lie algebra.
We have the canonical projections

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}:\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}\quad(\ell^{\prime}>\ell).

(0.1.21)

Thus, we have the completion of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}\simeq\prod_{n\in N^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{n}\quad\text{(as a vector space)}

(0.1.22)

with the canonical projections
\pi_{\ell}:\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
(
\ell>0
).

For any Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
,
its completion
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{>\ell}=\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell},\quad\mathfrak{h}^{\leq\ell}=\mathfrak{h}/\mathfrak{h}^{>\ell},\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathfrak{h}^{\leq\ell}.

(0.1.23)

Next, we define the exponential group
G

for the Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}

in (
0.1.22
).
In fact, there are two equivalent ways to define it.

(a). For each nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}

in (
0.1.20
),
we define the associated exponential group

\displaystyle G^{\leq\ell}=\exp(\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell})

(0.1.24)

as follows:
As a set,
G^{\leq\ell}
is identified with
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}

with
\exp(X)\leftrightarrow X
,
and its product is defined by the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula

(e.g.,
[
Jac79
, §V.5]
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \exp(X)\exp(Y)\\
=&\ \exp\biggl(X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{1}{12}[X,[X,Y]]-\frac{1}{12}[Y,[X,Y]]+\cdots\biggr),\end{split}

(0.1.25)

where we do not need the explicit form of the higher order
commutators here.
Originally, the BCH formula is a product formula
for the exponential element
e^{X}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}X^{k}/k!
in the situation where
e^{X}
makes sense.
Here, the product is well-defined due to the nilpotency of
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
.
Moreover, it is compatible with the canonical projections

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}:G^{\leq\ell^{\prime}}\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell}\quad(\ell^{\prime}>\ell).

(0.1.26)

Thus, by the inverse limit we obtain a group

\displaystyle G=G_{\mathfrak{s}}=G_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}:=\lim_{\longleftarrow}G^{\leq\ell}

(0.1.27)

with the canonical projections
\pi_{\ell}:G\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell}
(
\ell>0
).
Let
G^{>\ell}:=\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell}
.
When we discuss
G^{\leq\ell}
, we often work with elements in
G

modulo
G^{>\ell}
.

(b).
Alternatively, we may directly define

\displaystyle G:=\exp(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}),

(0.1.28)

where the infinite sum in the BCH formula (
0.1.25
) makes sense
for
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
again due to the nilpotency of
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
.
An element
\exp(X)
(
X\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
) is identified
with an element
(\exp(\pi_{\ell}(X)))_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
of
G
in (
0.1.27
).

Here we call
G
the
structure group
for the forthcoming scattering diagrams.

We remark that the group
G
depends only on
N
,
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
,
and
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
among the data from
\Gamma
and
\mathfrak{s}
.
In view of the decomposition (
0.1.5
), they corresponds to the matrix
\Omega
.
Thus, one can take a common
G
for those
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})

where
N
,
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
,
and
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
(and
\Omega
) are fixed but

N^{\circ}
and
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}
(and
\Delta
) are taken differently.

For any
g=\exp(X)\in G
and any

c\in\mathbbm{k}
,
a power
g^{c}
of
g
is defined by

\displaystyle g^{c}:=\exp(cX).

(0.1.29)

In particular, for any
c\in\mathbb{Q}
, the fractional power
g^{c}
of
g
is unambiguously defined.

We may consider an
infinite
product in
G
as follows.
Suppose that there is a countable and linearly ordered sequence
(g_{i})_{i\in I}
(
g_{i}\in G
).
Suppose that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, there are only finitely many
i\in I

such that
\pi_{\ell}(g_{i})\neq\mathrm{id}
.
Let
p_{\ell}
be the product of those
g_{i}
such that

\pi_{\ell}(g_{i})\neq\mathrm{id}
along its linear order, say, from left to right.
Then,
\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}p_{\ell}
yields an element in
G
,
which is the infinite product
{\prod}_{i\in I}g_{i}
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be any Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
,
and let
\mathfrak{h}^{\leq\ell}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
be the ones
in (
0.1.23
).
Then, the corresponding subgroup
H
of
G
is defined by

\displaystyle H^{\leq\ell}:=\exp(\mathfrak{h}^{\leq\ell}),\quad H:=\lim_{\longleftarrow}H^{\leq\ell}=\exp(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}).

(0.1.30)

The following subgroups of
G
are especially important.

Definition 0.1.5

(Parallel subgroup)
.

For each
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
, we define a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}:=\bigoplus_{j>0}\mathfrak{g}_{jn}.

(0.1.31)

This is abelian by (
0.1.18
) and the skew-symmetry of
(
0.1.1
).
Then,
we have an abelian subgroup

G_{n}^{\parallel}
of
G

corresponding to
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}^{\parallel}

as defined in (
0.1.30
).
We call
G_{n}^{\parallel}
the
parallel subgroup
of
n
.

From the BCH formula (
0.1.25
),
we obtain the
Zassenhaus formula

[
Mag54
]
, which is regarded as the dual of
the BCH formula:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\exp(X+Y)\\
=&\exp(X)\exp(Y)\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]\biggr)\exp\biggl(\frac{1}{3}[Y,[X,Y]]+\frac{1}{6}[X,[X,Y]]\biggr)\cdots,\end{split}

(0.1.32)

where again the explicit form of higher commutators are not necessary,
and the infinite product makes sense in
G
.
Together with the BCH formula,
it also implies the useful formulas

\displaystyle\exp(X)\exp(Y)

\displaystyle=\exp(Y)\exp(X)\exp([X,Y])C

(0.1.33)

\displaystyle=\exp(Y)\exp([X,Y])C^{\prime}\exp(X).

(0.1.34)

where
C
and
C^{\prime}
are (possibly infinite) products of the exponentials of higher commutators
of
X
and
Y
.

0.1.3
Representations of
G

Let us introduce two kinds of representations of
G
,
which build a bridge between scattering diagrams and cluster algebras.

Let us recall some basic facts on derivations
[
Jac79
, §I.2]
.
Let
A
be a (not necessarily associative) algebra over
\mathbbm{k}
.
A linear map
D:A\rightarrow A
is a
derivation
of
A

if
D(ab)=D(a)b+aD(b)
holds for any
a,b\in A
.
For any derivations
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
of
A
,

[D_{1},D_{2}]:=D_{1}D_{2}-D_{2}D_{1}
is also a derivation.
Thus, we have the Lie algebra
\mathrm{Der}(A)
consisting of all derivations of
A

called the
derivation (Lie) algebra
.
For a derivation
D
,
suppose that the infinite sum
\exp(D)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}D^{k}/k!
is
well-defined as a linear map on
A
. Then,
\exp(D)
is an algebra automorphism
of
A
.

(a).
y
-representation
.
We consider a monoid

\displaystyle Q=Q_{\mathfrak{s}}:=

\displaystyle\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}e_{i}\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\biggr\}=N^{+}\sqcup\{0\}\subset N.

(0.1.35)

Let
\mathbbm{k}[Q]
be the monoid algebra of
Q
over
\mathbbm{k}
.
Let
J
be the maximal ideal of
\mathbbm{k}[Q]

generated by
N^{+}=Q\setminus\{0\}
,
and let

\displaystyle\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathbbm{k}[Q]/J^{\ell}

(0.1.36)

be the completion with respect to
J
.
We express any element
f\in\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]

as a formal power series in a symbol
y
as

\displaystyle f=\sum_{n\in Q}c_{n}y^{n}.

(0.1.37)

Let
\mathrm{Der}(\mathbbm{k}[[Q]])
be the derivation algebra of
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]
.

For each
n\in N^{+}
,
we define a derivation
\tilde{X}_{n}
on
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]

by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{n}(y^{n^{\prime}}):=\{n,n^{\prime}\}y^{n^{\prime}+n}\quad(n^{\prime}\in Q).

(0.1.38)

Proposition 0.1.6

(cf.
[
GHKK18
, §1.1]
)
.

The map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho_{y}:&\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Der}(\mathbbm{k}[[Q]])\\
&\displaystyle X=\sum_{n\in N^{+}}c_{n}X_{n}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\tilde{X}=\sum_{n\in N^{+}}c_{n}\tilde{X}_{n},\end{matrix}

(0.1.39)

yields a Lie algebra homomorphism,
where the sum could be infinite.
Moreover, if
\Gamma
is nondegenerate,
it is injective.

Proof.

First, we regard
\rho_{y}
in (
0.1.39
) with finite sum as a map
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathrm{Der}(\mathbbm{k}[Q])
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{X}_{n_{1}}\tilde{X}_{n_{2}}(y^{n})&=\tilde{X}_{n_{1}}(\{n_{2},n\}y^{n+n_{2}})\\
&=(\{n_{1},n\}+\{n_{1},n_{2}\})\{n_{2},n\}y^{n+n_{2}+n_{1}}.\end{split}

(0.1.40)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\tilde{X}_{n_{1}},\tilde{X}_{n_{2}}](y^{n})&=\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\{n_{1}+n_{2},n\}y^{n+n_{1}+n_{2}}\\
&=\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\tilde{X}_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(y^{n}).\end{split}

(0.1.41)

Therefore,
\rho_{y}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathrm{Der}(\mathbbm{k}[Q])
is a Lie algebra homomorphism.
We show that it is extended to the one in (
0.1.39
).
First, we note that

\mathfrak{g}(J^{\ell+1})\subset J^{\ell+2}
.
Thus,

\mathfrak{g}
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[Q]^{\leq\ell}:=\mathbbm{k}[Q]/J^{\ell+1}
.
Next,
we have

\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell}(\mathbbm{k}[Q])\subset J^{\ell+1}
.
Thus,
it is extended to the action of

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Moreover, this action is compatible with
projections

\pi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}:\mathbbm{k}[Q]^{\leq\ell^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbbm{k}[Q]^{\leq\ell}

for
\ell^{\prime}>\ell
.
Therefore,

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]
.
If
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is non-degenerate,

\tilde{X}_{n}
(
n\in N^{+}
) in (
0.1.38
) are linearly independent.
Thus,
\rho_{y}
is injective.
∎

Remark 0.1.7
.

The above representation
\rho_{y}

is equivalent to the direct sum of the adjoint and the trivial representations of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.

For any
X\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
,
let
\tilde{X}:={\rho}_{y}(X)
.
We define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\tilde{X}^{k}\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]),

(0.1.42)

which is well-defined by the same reason in the proof of Proposition
0.1.6
.
Since
\tilde{X}
is a derivation,
\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X})

is an algebra automorphism of
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]

as we mentioned.
Then, we have the following group homomorphism,
where we abuse the symbol
\rho_{y}
, for simplicity.

Proposition 0.1.8
.

We have a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho_{y}:&G&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbbm{k}[[Q]])\\
&\exp(X)&\mapsto&\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}).\end{matrix}

(0.1.43)

Moreover, if
\Gamma
is nondegenerate,
it is injective.

Proof.

The map
\rho_{y}
is a group homomorphism,
because the exponential (
0.1.42
) also satisfies the
same
BCH formula as mentioned.
Suppose that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.
Let us show that
\rho_{y}
is injective.
Assume that
\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X})=\mathrm{id}
.
Then,
by the action (
0.1.38
),
we have
\tilde{X}=0
.
Then,
X=0
in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
by the injectivity of

\rho_{y}
in Proposition
0.1.6
.
Thus,
\exp(X)=\mathrm{id}
.
∎

The representation
\rho_{y}
is
closely related with the mutations of
y
-variables
in
(
0.1.7
)
as we will see soon.
Thus, we call it the
y
-representation
of
G
.

(b).
x
-representation
.
Here we assume that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate
from the beginning.
Then, thanks to Lemma
0.1.4
, the map
p^{*}
in (
0.1.8
)
is injective.
In particular,
p^{*}(e_{1})
, …,
p^{*}(e_{r})
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent.
We define
a monoid

\displaystyle P=P_{\mathfrak{s}}:=

\displaystyle\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}p^{*}(e_{i})\biggm|a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\biggr\}=p^{*}(Q)\subset M^{\circ}.

(0.1.44)

Let
\mathbbm{k}[P]
the monoid algebra of
P
over
\mathbbm{k}
,
and
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
be the completion by the maximal ideal of

\mathbbm{k}[P]
generated by
P\setminus\{0\}
.
(Note that, if
\Gamma
is degenerate, we cannot define
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
.)
We express any element
f\in\mathbbm{k}[[P]]

as a formal power series in a symbol
x
as

\displaystyle f=\sum_{m\in P}c_{m}x^{m}.

(0.1.45)

Then,
in parallel to (
0.1.38
),
for each
n\in N^{+}
,
we define a derivation
\tilde{X}_{n}
on
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{n}(x^{m}):=\langle n,m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n)}\quad(m\in P).

(0.1.46)

Then, repeating the same argument for
\rho_{y}
, we obtain
the following group homomorphism.

Proposition 0.1.9
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.
Then,
we have an injective group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho_{x}:&G&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbbm{k}[[P]])\\
&\exp(X)&\mapsto&\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}).\end{matrix}

(0.1.47)

The representation
\rho_{x}
is
closely related with the mutations of
x
-variables
in
(
0.1.7
)
as we will see soon.
Thus, we call it the
x
-representation
of
G
.

Even though a faithful representation of
G

is desirable in some situation,
it is too restrictive to assume that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate
in view of cluster algebra theory.
We will treat this problem later in Section

0.4
.

0.1.4
Dilogarithm elements and pentagon relation

As an application of the actions of
G
in the previous subsection,
we show a remarkable relation among certain distinguished elements in
G
.

Let
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
be the
Euler dilogarithm

[
Lew81
]
defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{j}}{j^{2}}=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\log(1-y)}{y}\,dy.

(0.1.48)

It has the following key property:

\displaystyle x\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}x^{j}=\log(1+x).

(0.1.49)

Having this in mind, we introduce the following elements of
G
.

Definition 0.1.10

(Dilogarithm element)
.

For any
n\in N^{+}
,
let

\displaystyle\Psi[n]:=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}X_{jn}\Biggr)\in G_{n_{0}}^{\parallel},

(0.1.50)

where
n_{0}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
is the unique one such that
n=tn_{0}
for some integer
t>0
.
We call
\Psi[n]
the
dilogarithm element
for
n
.

Remark 0.1.11
.

It is “only formally” expressed as

\displaystyle\Psi[n]=\exp(-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-X_{n})),

(0.1.51)

if we interpret
X_{n}^{j}
as
X_{jn}
.
(Caution:
X_{n}^{j}
and
X_{jn}
act differently on
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]
and
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
.)

The elements
\Psi[n]
are introduced because of the following property.

Proposition 0.1.12

(Cf.
[
GHKK18
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

(a).
Under the
y
-representation
\rho_{y}
,
\Psi[n]
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[[Q]]
as

\displaystyle\Psi[n](y^{n^{\prime}})=y^{n^{\prime}}(1+y^{n})^{\{n,n^{\prime}\}}\quad(n^{\prime}\in Q).

(0.1.52)

(b). Suppose that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.
Then, under the
x
-representation
\rho_{x}
,
\Psi[n]
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[[P]]
as

\displaystyle\Psi[n](x^{m})=x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{\langle n,m\rangle}\quad(m\in P).

(0.1.53)

Proof.

Since the calculations are parallel, we concentrate on the case (b).

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi[n](x^{m})&=x^{m}\exp\Biggl(\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}x^{jp^{*}(n)}\langle jn,m\rangle\Biggr)\\
&=x^{m}\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}x^{jp^{*}(n)}\Biggr)^{\langle n,m\rangle}\\
&=x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{\langle n,m\rangle}.\end{split}

(0.1.54)

∎

Observe that the calculation in (
0.1.54
)
is parallel to the derivation of
the formula
(
0.1.49
) by the power series calculation.
The above automorphisms (
0.1.52
)
and (
0.1.53
) are identified with the automorphism part of the Fock-Goncharov decomposition of mutations
of
y
-variables (coefficients)
and
x
-variables (cluster variables)
in cluster algebras, respectively, in
[
FG09
, §2.1]
.

The dilogarithm elements are generators of
G

in the following sense.

Proposition 0.1.13
.

The group
G
is generated by
\Psi[tn]^{c}

(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},c\in\mathbbm{k})

admitting the infinite product.

Proof.

By inverting the expression (
0.1.50
),
one can express
\exp(X_{n})
(
n\in N^{+}
) as an infinite product of

\Psi[tn]^{c}
(
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
c\in\mathbb{Q}
).
∎

The dilogarithm elements satisfy the following remarkable relations.

Proposition 0.1.14
.

Let
n,n^{\prime}\in N^{+}
.
The following relations hold in
G
.

(a). If
\{n^{\prime},n\}=0
,
for any
c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{k}
,

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}\Psi[n]^{c}=\Psi[n]^{c}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}.

(0.1.55)

(b). (Pentagon relation
[
GHKK18
, Example 1.14]
for
c=1
.)
If
\{n^{\prime},n\}=c

(c\in\mathbb{Q},c\neq 0)
,

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{1/c}\Psi[n]^{1/c}=\Psi[n]^{1/c}\Psi[n+n^{\prime}]^{1/c}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{1/c}.

(0.1.56)

Proof.

(a).
This is clear from (
0.1.18
).
(b).
Let us consider the rank 2 sublattice
N^{\prime}
of
N
generated by
n
and
n^{\prime}
.
By the assumption
\{n^{\prime},n\}\neq 0
, the form
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
restricted on
N^{\prime}
is nondegenerate.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgroup of
G
corresponding to
N^{\prime}
.
Similarly, we consider a monoid

\displaystyle P^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\{ap^{*}(n)+a^{\prime}p^{*}(n^{\prime})\mid a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\subset M^{\circ}.

(0.1.57)

Since the relation (
0.1.56
) involves only elements in
G^{\prime}
,
one can prove it
by the
x
-representation of
G^{\prime}
on
\mathbbm{k}[[P^{\prime}]]
, which is faithful.
The left hand side is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \Psi[n^{\prime}]^{1/c}\Psi[n]^{1/c}(x^{m})\\
&=\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{1/c}(x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{\langle n,m\rangle/c})\\
&=x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})}))^{\langle n,m\rangle/c}.\end{split}

(0.1.58)

The right hand side is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \Psi[n]^{1/c}\Psi[n+n^{\prime}]^{1/c}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{1/c}(x^{m})\\
&=\Psi[n]^{1/c}\Psi[n+n^{\prime}]^{1/c}(x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c})\\
&=\Psi[n]^{1/c}(x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})})^{\langle n+n^{\prime},m\rangle/c}\\
&\qquad\times(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})})^{-1})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c})\\
&=\Psi[n]^{1/c}(x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})})^{\langle n,m\rangle/c}(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})}+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c})\\
&=x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{\langle n,m\rangle/c}(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{-1})^{\langle n,m\rangle/c}\\
&\qquad\times(1+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{-1}+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)})^{-1})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c}\\
&=x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n)}+x^{p^{*}(n+n^{\prime})})^{\langle n,m\rangle/c}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle/c}.\end{split}

(0.1.59)

Thus, two expressions agree.
∎

Remark 0.1.15
.

(a).
The above calculation for
c=1
is essentially the same for the
pentagon periodicity

of
x
-variables for a cluster algebra of type
A_{2}
in
[
FZ02
]
.
Alternatively,
one can also work with the
y
-representation.
Then, the calculation for
c=1
is essentially
the same for the pentagon periodicity
of
y
-variables for a cluster algebra of type
A_{2}
in
[
FZ02
]
.

(b). The case
c\neq 1
follows from the case
c=1
by Proposition

0.1.17
.

0.1.5
Equivalence of fixed data and normalization factor

There is some redundancy for choosing a fixed data.
Let us start with a simple situation.

Definition 0.1.16

(Rescaling of fixed data)
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}

be a pair of fixed data.
We say that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a
rescaling of
\Gamma

if there is some rational number
\lambda>0
such that
the following relations hold:

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle=N,

(0.1.60)

\displaystyle\{n_{1},n_{2}\}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda\{n_{1},n_{2}\},

(0.1.61)

\displaystyle(N^{\circ})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}N^{\circ},

(0.1.62)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\delta_{i},

(0.1.63)

\displaystyle(M^{\circ})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lambda M^{\circ}.

(0.1.64)

The exchange matrix
B
associated with
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})

in (
0.1.4
)
is invariant under the above rescaling, namely,

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{ij}=\{\delta^{\prime}_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}^{\prime}=\lambda\{\lambda^{-1}\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}=b_{ij}.

(0.1.65)

Let
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
be a seed for
\Gamma
.
Then,
\mathfrak{s}
is also regarded as a seed for
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
be the corresponding Lie algebra and group
for
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}
.

Proposition 0.1.17
.

We have a Lie algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\phi_{\lambda}:&\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}&\rightarrow&\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\\
&X^{\prime}_{n}&\mapsto&\lambda X_{n}\end{matrix}

(0.1.66)

and a group isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\phi_{\lambda}:&G^{\prime}&\rightarrow&G\\
&\exp(X^{\prime}_{n})&\mapsto&\exp(\lambda X_{n}).\end{matrix}

(0.1.67)

Proof.

This is
due to the relation (
0.1.61
) and the commutation relation (
0.1.18
).
∎

Definition 0.1.18

(Normalization factor)
.

For any
n\in N^{+}
,
let
\delta(n)
be the smallest positive rational number
such that
\delta(n)n\in N^{\circ}
.
We call
\delta(n)
the
normalization factor
of
n
with respect to
N^{\circ}
.

Example 0.1.19
.

(a). For the seed
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
under consideration,
we have
\delta(e_{i})=\delta_{i}
.

(b). For any
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
and any integer
t>0
,
we have

\displaystyle\delta(tn)=\delta(n)/t,

(0.1.68)

because
\delta(tn)tn=\delta(n)n
.
Also,
\delta(n)
is an integer.
Otherwise, let
\delta(n)=r/s
be the irreducible rational expression with
s\neq 1
.
Then, we have
n,(r/s)n\in N
.
Thus,
(1/s)n\in N
.
This contradicts
that

n
is primitive.

Proposition 0.1.20
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be as above.
For any
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
,
let
g\in G_{n}^{\parallel}
and
g^{\prime}\in(G^{\prime})_{n}^{\parallel}

with

\displaystyle g=\exp\Biggl(\delta(n)\sum_{j>0}c_{j}X_{jn}\Biggr),\quad g^{\prime}=\exp\Biggl(\delta^{\prime}(n)\sum_{j>0}c^{\prime}_{j}X^{\prime}_{jn}\Biggr),

(0.1.69)

where
\delta(n)
and
\delta^{\prime}(n)
are the normalization factors
with respect to
N^{\circ}
and
(N^{\circ})^{\prime}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\phi_{\lambda}(g^{\prime})=g.

(0.1.70)

if and only
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
for any
j>0
.

Proof.

This follows from (
0.1.66
)
and the transformation of the normalization factors

\delta^{\prime}(n)=\lambda^{-1}\delta(n)
by (
0.1.62
).
∎

In view of Proposition
0.1.20
,
it is natural to express an element
g\in G_{n}^{\parallel}
as

\displaystyle g=g_{0}^{\delta(n)},

(0.1.71)

where
g_{0}=g^{1/\delta(n)}
.
We call (
0.1.71
) the
normalized form
of
g
.
Then, the coefficients
c_{j}
of the expansion

g_{0}=\exp(\,\sum_{j>0}c_{j}X_{jn})

are
invariant
under the rescaling of
\Gamma
.

Example 0.1.21
.

For
\Psi[n]
in (
0.1.50
) with
n=tn_{0}
therein, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{\lambda}(\Psi^{\prime}[n]^{\delta^{\prime}(n_{0})})=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n_{0})}.

(0.1.72)

Thus, we also have

\displaystyle\phi_{\lambda}(\Psi^{\prime}[n]^{\delta^{\prime}(n)})=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}.

(0.1.73)

These elements play the main role in the forthcoming CSDs.

Now we consider a more general situation.

Definition 0.1.22
.

Let
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
be a pair of a fixed data and a seed,
and let
B_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}

be the associated matrix in (
0.1.4
).
We say that
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
and
(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})

are
B
-equivalent

if
B_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}=B_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
.

In Section
0.1.1

we have seen that, for a given skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
,
there is a (not unique) pair
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})

such that
B=B_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
.
Let us show that the associated group
G_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}

is uniquely determined from
B
up to isomorphism.
This fact is natural in view of
the correspondence to cluster algebras.

Proposition 0.1.23
.

If
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{s})
and
(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
are
B
-equivalent,
then the associated groups
G_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}
and
G_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}

are isomorphic.

Proof.

Let
B=B_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}=B_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
.
We say that an
n\times n
matrix
A
is
indecomposable

if there is no pair
I,J\subset\{1,\dots,r\}
,
I\sqcup J=\{1,\dots,r\}
,

I,J\neq\emptyset
such that
a_{ij}=0
for any
i\in I
and
j\in J
.
Suppose that
B
is decomposed into

B_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus B_{p}

up to simultaneous permutation of rows and columns,
where
B_{i}
are indecomposable submatrices of
B
.
Accordingly, the groups
G=G_{\Gamma,\mathfrak{s}}

and
G^{\prime}=G_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
are factorized into the corresponding
subgroups
G=\prod_{i=1}^{p}G_{i}
and
G^{\prime}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}G^{\prime}_{i}
.
Thus, we only need to prove the claim when
B
is indecomposable.
Then, it is well known and easy to show that a (left) skew-symmetrizer of
B
is
unique up to a positive rational number.
This implies that
\delta_{i}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{i}
are related by

\delta^{\prime}_{i}=\lambda^{-1}\delta_{i}
with a common multiple
\lambda^{-1}
.
Let us identify
e_{i}
with
e^{\prime}_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,r
) and
N
with
N^{\prime}
.
Now we are in the situation of Definition
0.1.16
.
Therefore,
G
and
G^{\prime}
are isomorphic
by Proposition
0.1.17
.
∎

The above proof also tells that
the parallel results to
Proposition
0.1.20
and Example
0.1.21
hold in the situation
of Proposition
0.1.23
.

0.1.6
Formulas for dilogarithm elements

Let us present some useful formulas for dilogarithm elements.

Proposition 0.1.24
.

Let
n,n^{\prime}\in N^{+}
.

(a).
(
[
GHKK18
, Lemma C.8]
.)
If
\{\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},n\}=\pm\alpha

(\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
, then
the following equality holds for
the
x
-representation
on
\mathbbm{k}[[P^{\prime}]]
for
P^{\prime}
in (
0.1.57
):

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\pm\delta(n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle X_{n}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\mp\delta(n^{\prime})}=\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{j}X_{n+jn^{\prime}}.

(0.1.74)

(b). If
\{\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},n\}=\alpha
and
\{\delta(n)n,n^{\prime}\}=-\beta

(\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
,
then

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}\Psi[\beta n+n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}C\Psi[n+\alpha n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n)}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})},

(0.1.75)

where
C
is a (possibly infinite) product of
\Psi[n^{\prime\prime}]^{c^{\prime\prime}}

(c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q})

such that
n^{\prime\prime}=j_{1}n+j_{2}n^{\prime}

(j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
with

\alpha^{-1}<j_{1}/j_{2}<\beta
.

Proof.

(a).
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ X_{n}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\mp\delta(n^{\prime})}(x^{m})\\
&=X_{n}(x^{m}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle})\\
&=\langle n,m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n)}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle}\\
&\quad+\alpha\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n^{\prime}+n)}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle-1},\end{split}

(0.1.76)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\mp\delta(n^{\prime})}\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{j}X_{n+jn^{\prime}}(x^{m})\\
&=\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\mp\delta(n^{\prime})}\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{j}\langle n+jn^{\prime},m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n+jn^{\prime})}\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{j}\langle n+jn^{\prime},m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n+jn^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle-\alpha}\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{j}\langle n,m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n+jn^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle-\alpha}\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{\alpha}\alpha\binom{\alpha-1}{j-1}\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n+jn^{\prime})}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle-\alpha}\\
&=\langle n,m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n)}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle}\\
&\quad+\alpha\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle x^{m+p^{*}(n^{\prime}+n)}(1+x^{p^{*}(n^{\prime})})^{\mp\langle\delta(n^{\prime})n^{\prime},m\rangle-1}.\end{split}

(0.1.77)

Thus, they coincide.

(b).
As in the proof of Proposition
0.1.14
, it is enough to prove it for
the
x
-representation
on
\mathbbm{k}[[P^{\prime}]]
for
P^{\prime}
in (
0.1.57
).
For any
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we obtain from (
0.1.74
) that

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle X_{tn}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{-\delta(n^{\prime})}=X_{tn}+\sum_{j=1}^{t\alpha-1}\binom{t\alpha}{j}X_{tn+jn^{\prime}}+X_{t(n+\alpha n^{\prime})}.

(0.1.78)

Taking the sum and exponential in accordance with (
0.1.50
),
then using the Zassenhaus formula (
0.1.32
) and Proposition
0.1.13
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{-\delta(n^{\prime})}=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}C_{1}\Psi[n+\alpha n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n)},

(0.1.79)

where
C_{1}
is a (possibly infinite) product of
\Psi[n^{\prime\prime}]^{c^{\prime\prime}}

(c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q})

such that
n^{\prime\prime}=j_{1}n+j_{2}n^{\prime}

(j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
with

0<j_{2}/j_{1}<\alpha
.
Similarly, by exchange the role of
n
and
n^{\prime}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi[n]^{-\delta(n)}

\displaystyle\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}=\Psi[\beta n+n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})}C_{2}\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{\delta(n^{\prime})},

(0.1.80)

where
C_{2}
is a (possibly infinite) product of
\Psi[n^{\prime\prime}]^{c^{\prime\prime}}

(c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q})

such that
n^{\prime\prime}=j_{1}n+j_{2}n^{\prime}

(j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
with

0<j_{1}/j_{2}<\beta
.
Combining (
0.1.79
) and (
0.1.80
),
we obtain (
0.1.75
).
∎

Notes

The contents are mostly taken from
[
GHKK18
, §1.1]

and partly from
[
KS14
, §2.1]
.
The
y
-representation of
G

in Section
0.1.4
appeared in
[
GHK15
, §2]
.
The pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) generalizes the
one in
[
GHKK18
, Example 1.14]
for
c=1
.
The equivalence of fixed data in Section
0.1.5

is distilled from the
change of lattice trick
in
[
GHKK18
, Appendix C.3]
.
Proposition
0.1.24
(b) is due to us.

0.2
Consistent scattering diagrams

In this section we introduce and study consistent scattering diagrams.
In particular, we show that there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between
consistent scattering diagrams and elements in the structure group
G
.

0.2.1
Scattering diagrams

We continue to fix a seed
\mathfrak{s}
for a given (possibly degenerate) fixed data
\Gamma
.

For any
n\in N
,
n\neq 0
, the rational hyperplane

n^{\perp}
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
that is orthogonal to
n
is defined by

\displaystyle n^{\perp}:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle n,z\rangle=0\}.

(0.2.1)

Definition 0.2.1

(Cone)
.

For
m_{1},\dots,m_{s}\in M^{\circ}
, let

\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma(m_{1},\dots,m_{s}):=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{s}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}.

(0.2.2)

We also set
\sigma(\emptyset)=\{0\}
.
We call
\sigma
a
convex rational polyhedral cone
, or simply, a
cone

in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We say that a cone
\sigma
is
strongly convex
if
\sigma\cap(-\sigma)=\{0\}
.

Definition 0.2.2

(Wall)
.

A
wall
for a seed
\mathfrak{s}

is a triplet
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{n}
,
where

•

n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,

•

\mathfrak{d}\subset n^{\perp}
is a
(not necessarily strongly convex) cone
of codimension 1 in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
,

•

g\in G_{n}^{\parallel}
.

We call
n
,
\mathfrak{d}
,
g

the
normal vector
,
the
support
,
the
wall element

of a wall
\mathbf{w}
, respectively.

Informally, the support
\mathfrak{d}
is also referred to as a
wall
;
for example, we say “A curve in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
crosses a wall.”
The normal vector
n
is uniquely specified from the data
\mathfrak{d}
.
So, we may omit it as
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)
when it is not necessary to indicate it explicitly.

Definition 0.2.3

(Scattering diagram)
.

A
scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}
for a seed
\mathfrak{s}

is a collection of walls

\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},g_{{\lambda}})_{n_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

for
\mathfrak{s}
,
where
\Lambda
is a
finite or countably infinite index set, satisfying the following

finiteness condition
:

•

For each integer
\ell>0
, there are only finitely many walls
such that
\pi_{\ell}(g_{{\lambda}})\neq\mathrm{id}
,
where
\pi_{\ell}:G\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell}
is the canonical projection.

For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
and any integer
\ell>0
,
let
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
be the collection of walls
obtained from
\mathfrak{D}

by removing all walls such that
\pi_{\ell}(g_{{\lambda}})=\mathrm{id}
.
We call
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
the
reduction
of
\mathfrak{D}
at degree
\ell
.
By the finiteness condition,
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
has only finitely many walls.

We give some related notions.

Definition 0.2.4

(Support/Singular locus)
.

For a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}=\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},g_{{\lambda}})_{n_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
,
the
support
and the
singular locus
of
\mathfrak{D}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},

(0.2.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cup\bigcup_{\scriptstyle\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\atop\scriptstyle\dim\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\,=\,r-2}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

(0.2.4)

We note that, if
\dim\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}<r-2
,
then it is contained in
\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cup\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
.

Definition 0.2.5

(Admissible curve)
.

A curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
is

admissible
for a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

The end points of
\gamma
are in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.

•

It is a smooth curve, and it intersects
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
transversally.

•

\gamma
does not intersect
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Definition 0.2.6

(Path-ordered product)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any scattering diagram.
For any admissible curve
\gamma
,
we define an element
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\in G
as follows:
For each integer
\ell>0
, the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
at
\ell
has only finitely many walls.
Suppose that

\gamma=\gamma(t)
crosses walls
\mathbf{w}_{i}=(\mathfrak{d}_{i},g_{i})_{n_{i}}

(
i=1,\dots,s
)
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}

in this order at
t=t_{i}
with

\displaystyle 0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\cdots\leq t_{s}<1.

(0.2.5)

Since
\gamma(t_{i})\notin\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
,
when
\gamma
crosses multiple walls at a time,
these walls have a common normal vector.
We define the
intersection sign

\epsilon_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,s
) by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\langle n_{i},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})\rangle<0,\\
-1&\langle n_{i},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})\rangle>0,\\
\end{cases}

(0.2.6)

where
\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})
is the velocity vector of
\gamma(t)
at
t_{i}
.
Now, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}:=

\displaystyle\ g_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\cdots g_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},

(0.2.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}:=

\displaystyle\ \lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}\in G.

(0.2.8)

We call
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}

the
path-ordered product
(of wall elements in
\mathfrak{D}
)
along
\gamma
.

Note that
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
only depends on the homotopy class of
\gamma

in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Definition 0.2.7

(Equivalence)
.

Two scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
for a common seed
\mathfrak{s}
are

equivalent
if, for any curve
\gamma
that is admissible for both
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, the equality

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}}
holds.

Definition 0.2.8

(Generality)
.

We say that
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
is
general

if there is at most one rational hyperplane
n^{\perp}
(
n\in N
,
n\neq 0
) such that

z\in n^{\perp}
.

Lemma 0.2.9
.

(a). The set of all general points is dense in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(b). For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
,
any point
z\in\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
is not general.

Proof.

(a). This is clear.
(b). Suppose that
z\in\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
for some
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
.
Since
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
is a cone of codimension 1,
z
belongs to a face of

\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
of codimension 2,
which lies in the intersection of two rational hyperplanes.
Thus,
z
is not general.
For
z\in\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
with

\dim\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}=r-2
,
the claim is clear.
∎

Definition 0.2.10

(Total wall element at
z
)
.

For any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}):=\prod_{\lambda:\,z\in\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}}g_{{\lambda}}\in\begin{cases}G_{n_{0}}^{\parallel}&\text{$z\in n^{\perp}_{0}$ for some unique $n_{0}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}$},\\
\{\mathrm{id}\}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(0.2.9)

The (possibly infinite) product is well-defined due to the finiteness condition
of
\mathfrak{D}
.
We call it the
total wall element of
\mathfrak{D}
at
z
.

The condition for the equivalence
reduces to the following local condition.

Lemma 0.2.11

(
[
GHKK18
, Lemma 1.9]
)
.

Two scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
are equivalent
if and only if
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
holds for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Consider the reductions
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\ell}
at
\ell
.
Note that both have only finitely many walls.
Then, thanks to Lemma
0.2.9
,
the equivalence condition reduces to
the following condition:

•

For any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}

and for any admissible curve
\gamma

that intersects walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\ell}
only at
z
,
the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.10)

Under the situation, we have
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}=g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})^{\epsilon}
with the intersection sign
\epsilon
.
Therefore, the above equality is written as

g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})\equiv g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\ell})
mod
G^{>\ell}
.
Since
\ell
is arbitrary,
we have
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})
.
∎

Definition 0.2.12

(Trivial scattering diagram)
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is
trivial
if
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=\mathrm{id}

for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
.

By Lemma
0.2.11
, a scattering diagram is trivial if and only if
it is equivalent to the empty scattering diagram
\emptyset
.

Finally, we introduce
the most crucial notion for scattering diagrams.

Definition 0.2.13

(Consistency)
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is
consistent
if for any admissible curve
\gamma

for
\mathfrak{D}
,
the associated path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
depends
only on
the end points of
\gamma
.

The following restatement is useful.

Lemma 0.2.14
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent if and only
if

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathrm{id}

(0.2.11)

holds for any admissible loop (i.e., closed curve)
\gamma
for
\mathfrak{D}
.

Proof.

This is proved by the standard argument for fundamental groups.
Assume that
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent.
Let
\gamma
be any admissible loop for
\mathfrak{D}
.
Split
\gamma
as
\gamma=(\gamma_{2})^{-1}\circ\gamma_{1}
,
where
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are admissible curves having the common ending points.
Then, by the consistency, we have
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}}
.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}})^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}=\mathrm{id}
holds. The converse is similar.
∎

0.2.2
Rank 2 examples: finite type

Based on the pentagon relation in Proposition
0.1.14
,
we construct some prototypical examples of consistent scattering diagrams of rank 2.
Let
e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\in M
be the dual basis of
e_{1},e_{2}\in N
.
Accordingly, let
f_{1}=e_{1}^{*}/\delta_{1},f_{2}=e_{2}^{*}/\delta_{2}\in M^{\circ}
be the dual basis of
\delta_{1}e_{1},\delta_{2}e_{2}\in N^{\circ}
.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the unit vectors.
We identify
N\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
e_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
,
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
f_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Then,
the canonical pairing

\langle n,z\rangle:N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is given
by the corresponding vectors
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{z}
as

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\mathbf{n}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}^{-1}&0\\
0&\delta_{2}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{z}.

(0.2.12)

Let

B=(b_{ij})

be the
2\times 2
integer matrix introduced in (
0.1.7
), namely,

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\delta_{1}a\\
\delta_{2}a&0\end{pmatrix},\quad a=\{e_{2},e_{1}\}.

(0.2.13)

(a). Type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
.
We start with the case
a=0
, so that
\Gamma
is degenerate.
Let
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
be arbitrary.
By (
0.1.55
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}.

(0.2.14)

This equality is naturally interpreted as
a consistent scattering diagram of rank 2 in Figure
1
(a).
Namely, it consists of two walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}})_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}.

(0.2.15)

The left hand side of the equality (
0.2.14
)
is the path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}

along
\gamma_{1}
,
while the right hand side is the one
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}}
along
\gamma_{2}
.

Now assume
a\neq 0
, so that
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.
Using the rescaling of fixed data
in Section
0.1.5

and interchanging
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, if necessary,
we may assume

\{e_{2},e_{1}\}=1

without loosing generality.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}&0\\
0&\delta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=\Delta\Omega.

(0.2.16)

Let
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the corresponding vector to
n\in N^{+}
.
Then, we have
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}=n^{\prime}_{2}n_{1}-n^{\prime}_{1}n_{2}
.

Here, we concentrate on the case
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\leq 3
.

(a)
A_{1}\times A_{1}
\textstyle{\gamma_{1}}
\textstyle{\gamma_{2}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{\delta_{1}}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{\delta_{2}}
(b)
A_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}
(c)
B_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}
(c’)
C_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}
(d)
G_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}

Figure 1:
Examples of consistent scattering diagrams of rank 2.
Column vectors with exponents represent the wall elements
\Psi[(n_{1},n_{2})]^{c}
.

(b). Type
A_{2}
. Let
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=1
.
Since
\{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{1}\}=1
,
we apply
the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=1
,
and we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]\Psi[(1,1)]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}].

(0.2.17)

This equality is naturally interpreted as
a consistent scattering diagram of rank 2 in Figure
1
(b).
Namely, it consists of three walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}])_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,1)])_{(1,1)}.

(0.2.18)

All normal vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1}
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}
,
(1,1)
exhaust the positive roots of the root system of type

A_{2}
.

(c). Type
B_{2}
and
C_{2}
.
Here we consider two cases,

\delta_{1}=1
,
\delta_{2}=2
(type
B_{2}
) and

\delta_{1}=2
,
\delta_{2}=1
(type
C_{2}
).
They are essentially the same.
Nevertheless,
we present both because they are useful to construct the rank 3 scattering diagrams
later
in Section
0.5.4
.
First, we consider the type
B_{2}
case.
We consider an element
\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]
.
For simplicity,
let us write
\Psi[(n_{1},n_{2})]
as
[n_{1},n_{2}]
.
Then,
by applying
the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=1
repeatedly for adjacent pairs

\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{n}

with
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.\end{split}

(0.2.19)

This equality is naturally interpreted as
a consistent scattering diagram in Figure
1
(c),
which consists of four walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(0.2.20)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[(1,1)]^{2})_{(1,1)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,2)])_{(1,2)}.

(0.2.21)

Some remarks are in order.
Firstly, by (
0.2.12
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2)\subset(1,1)^{\perp},\quad\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1)\subset(1,2)^{\perp}.

(0.2.22)

Secondly,
the exponent
c=1
or 2 of each wall element
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{c}
is the normalization factor
\delta(\mathbf{n})

in Definition
0.1.18
, where
N^{\circ}
is identified with the sublattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
generated by

(1,0)
and
(0,2)
.
Thirdly, the result is compatible with the formula (
0.1.75
).
All normal vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1}
,
\mathbf{e}_{2}
,
(1,1)
,
(1,2)
exhaust the positive roots of the root system of type

B_{2}
.
The type
C_{2}
case is similar,
and the corresponding scattering diagram is presented in Figure
1
(c’).

(d). Type
G_{2}
. Let
\delta_{1}=1
,
\delta_{2}=3
.
We consider an element
\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{3}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]
.
Again,
by applying
the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=1
repeatedly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\biggl(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\biggr)\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}.\end{split}

(0.2.23)

This equality is naturally interpreted as
a consistent scattering diagram in Figure
1
(d),
which consists of six walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{3})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(0.2.24)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-3),\Psi[(1,1)]^{3})_{(1,1)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[(2,3)])_{(2,3)},

(0.2.25)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2,-3),\Psi[(1,2)]^{3})_{(1,2)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,3)])_{(1,3)}.

(0.2.26)

The same remarks in the case (c) are applied as before.
The case
\delta_{1}=3
,
\delta_{2}=1
is similar.

These examples are particular cases of

cluster scattering diagrams
, which we are going to study.

0.2.3
Decompositions of
G
at general point

Let us begin with a general fact on nilpotent Lie algebras.

Lemma 0.2.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a nilpotent Lie algebra that has
a decomposition (as a vector space)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}

(0.2.27)

by its Lie subalgebras
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
.
Let
G=\exp(\mathfrak{g})
and
G_{i}=\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{i})

(i=1,2)

be the corresponding exponential groups
whose products are defined
by the BCH formula (
0.1.25
).
Then, we have the following decomposition of
G
:

\displaystyle G=G_{1}G_{2},\quad G_{1}\cap G_{2}=\{\mathrm{id}\}.

(0.2.28)

In other words, any element
g\in G
is uniquely factorized as

g=g_{1}g_{2}

(g_{i}\in G_{i})
.

Proof.

By the assumption (
0.2.27
),
any element
g\in G
is expressed as
g=\exp(X+Y)
,
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
,
Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}
.
Then, apply the Zassenhaus formula (
0.1.32
). If the right hand side of (
0.1.32
)
is already of the form
g_{1}g_{2}

(g_{i}\in G_{i})
, we are done.
Otherwise,
expand the terms
\exp(Z)

(Z\not\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{g}_{2})
therein
by (
0.1.32
) again,
and transpose the left-most pair
\exp(X_{2})\exp(X_{1})

(
X_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{i}
) in the opposite order by (
0.1.33
).
Continue the process until we end up with the expression

g_{1}g_{2}
,
g_{i}\in G_{i}
.
Due to the nilpotency of
\mathfrak{g}
, the process completes
in finitely many steps.
Thus,
G=G_{1}G_{2}
.
If
\exp(X_{1})=\exp(X_{2})
(
X_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{i}
),
we have
X_{1}=X_{2}
.
Then, we have
X_{1}=X_{2}=0
by (
0.2.27
).
Therefore,
G_{1}\cap G_{2}=\{\mathrm{id}\}
.
∎

We apply the above lemma to the following situation.
For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
of a fixed data
\Gamma
,
let
\mathfrak{g}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
G
be the ones in Section
0.1.2
.
Let
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
be any general point.
We introduce the
decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
at
z
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{z}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{z}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{z},

(0.2.29)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{z}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,z\rangle>0}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{z}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,z\rangle=0}\mathfrak{g}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{z}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,z\rangle<0}\mathfrak{g}_{n}.

(0.2.30)

By (
0.1.18
),

\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{z}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{z}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{z}

are Lie subagebras of
\mathfrak{g}
.
By the generality assumption of
z
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{z}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}_{n_{0}}^{\parallel}&\text{$z\in n^{\perp}_{0}$ for some unique $n_{0}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}$},\\
\{0\}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(0.2.31)

where

\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}
is the one in
(
0.1.31
).

Let

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}^{z}
,

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}^{z}
,

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}^{z}

be their completions,
and
let
G_{+}^{z}
,
G_{0}^{z}
,
G_{-}^{z}

be the corresponding subgroups of
G
.
In particular,

\displaystyle G_{0}^{z}=\begin{cases}G_{n_{0}}^{\parallel}&\text{$z\in n^{\perp}_{0}$ for some unique $n_{0}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}$},\\
\{0\}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(0.2.32)

Correspondingly, we have the
decomposition of
G
at
z

as follows.

Proposition 0.2.16

(
[
GHKK18
, Proof of Theorem 1.17]
)
.

For any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
we have

\displaystyle G=G_{+}^{z}G_{0}^{z}G_{-}^{z}

(0.2.33)

so that
any element
g
of
G
is
uniquely factorized as

\displaystyle g=g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z}g_{-}^{z}\quad(g_{+}^{z}\in G_{+}^{z},\ g_{0}^{z}\in G_{0}^{z},\ g_{-}^{z}\in G_{-}^{z}).

(0.2.34)

Proof.

Apply Lemma
0.2.15

to the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}=(\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{z})^{\leq\ell}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{z})^{\leq\ell}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{z})^{\leq\ell},

(0.2.35)

and we obtain the decomposition of
G^{\leq\ell}

\displaystyle G^{\leq\ell}=(G_{+}^{z})^{\leq\ell}(G_{0}^{z})^{\leq\ell}(G_{-}^{z})^{\leq\ell}

(0.2.36)

with the unique factorization property.
Moreover, the decomposition is compatible with the projection

\pi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}

in (
0.1.26
).
Then, taking the inverse limit, we obtain
(
0.2.33
).
The unique factorization property is preserved in
the inverse limit.
∎

By Proposition
0.2.16

and (
0.2.32
), for any
g\in G
and any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
we have a unique
g_{0}^{z}
such that

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}\in\begin{cases}G_{n_{0}}^{\parallel}&\text{$z\in n^{\perp}_{0}$ for some unique $n_{0}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}$},\\
\{\mathrm{id}\}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(0.2.37)

Compare it with (
0.2.9
).

\langle n,z\rangle<0
\langle n,z^{\prime}\rangle>0
n_{0}^{\perp}
n^{\perp}
z^{\prime}
z
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 2:
Decomposition at
z
is not constant on
n_{0}^{\perp}
.

What is important here is that, for a general
z\in n_{0}^{\perp}
,

g_{0}^{z}

varies discontinuously on the whole hyperplane
n_{0}^{\perp}
,
in general.
When
z
crosses the intersection
n_{0}^{\perp}\cap n^{\perp}
with

n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
other than
n_{0}
,
the sign of
\langle n,z\rangle
changes.
See Figure
2
.
Thus, the decomposition
(
0.2.33
) changes.

We are going to relate
the decomposition of
g\in G
at
z

with a consistent scattering diagram.
To begin with,
let us fix an integer
\ell>0
.
There are only finitely many hyperplanes

n^{\perp}
with

n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
.
They intersect each other, so that each hyperplane
is subdivided into cones.
Let
z
,
w_{1}
, and
w_{2}
be general points in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

as follows: (See Figure
3
.)

•

z
belongs to
n_{0}^{\perp}
for some unique
n_{0}\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
.

•

w_{1}
and
w_{2}
do not belong to
n^{\perp}
for any

n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
.

•

\langle n_{0},w_{1}\rangle>0
and
\langle n_{0},w_{2}\rangle<0
.

•

For any
n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
other than
n_{0}
,

\langle n,w_{i}\rangle>0
if and only if
\langle n,z\rangle>0
.

n_{0}^{\perp}
z
w_{1}
w_{2}
\mathcal{C}^{+}
\mathcal{C}^{-}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 3:
Configuration of
z
,
w_{1}
,
w_{2}

for Lemma
0.2.17
.

We have a key lemma.

Lemma 0.2.17

(
[
Bri17
, Lemma 3.2]
)
.

For any
g\in G
and
the above
z,w_{1},w_{2}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
we have

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}\equiv(g_{+}^{w_{2}})^{-1}g_{+}^{w_{1}}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.38)

Also, when the configuration of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are interchanged,
we have

\displaystyle(g_{0}^{z})^{-1}\equiv(g_{+}^{w_{2}})^{-1}g_{+}^{w_{1}}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.39)

Proof.

Consider the decomposition
g=g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z}g_{-}^{z}
at
z
.
Under the condition of
z
,
w_{1}
, and
w_{2}
,
the equality
g\equiv g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z}g_{-}^{z}
mod
G^{>\ell}
is interpreted also as
the decompositions
g\equiv(g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z})g_{-}^{z}
at
w_{1}

and
g\equiv g_{+}^{z}(g_{0}^{z}g_{-}^{z})
at
w_{2}
mod
G^{>\ell}
as well,
where the middle terms
g_{0}^{w_{1}}
and
g_{0}^{w_{2}}

are the identity mod
G^{>\ell}
.
Therefore,
g_{+}^{w_{1}}\equiv g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z}
and
g_{+}^{w_{2}}\equiv g_{+}^{z}
.
Thus, we have (
0.2.38
).
The formula (
0.2.39
) is obtained from (
0.2.38
) by taking the inverse.
∎

0.2.4
Construction of consistent scattering diagrams

For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
, we define
the
positive and negative orthants

\mathcal{C}^{\pm}
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+}:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle e_{i},z\rangle\geq 0\ (i=1,\dots,r)\},

(0.2.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{-}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-}:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle e_{i},z\rangle\leq 0\ (i=1,\dots,r)\}.

(0.2.41)

Lemma 0.2.18
.

For any wall for
\mathfrak{s}
,
its support
\mathfrak{d}
intersects

\mathcal{C}^{\pm}
only in the boundary of
\mathcal{C}^{\pm}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathfrak{d}\subset n^{\perp}
for its normal vector
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.
On the other hand,
for any
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,

\langle n,z\rangle>0
if
z\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
,
and
\langle n,z\rangle<0
if
z\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})
.
Thus,
\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\pm})=\emptyset
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram for
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
\gamma_{+-}
be any admissible curve for
\mathfrak{D}
with the initial point in

\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
and the final point in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})
.
By the consistency and Lemma
0.2.18
,
the element

\displaystyle g(\mathfrak{D}):=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{+-},\mathfrak{D}}\in G

(0.2.42)

is independent of the choice of such
\gamma_{+-}
.

Surprisingly, the single element
g(\mathfrak{D})
contains all essential information
of the whole consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

up to equivalence.

Proposition 0.2.19

(
[
GHKK18
, Proof of Theorem 1.17]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram.
Let
g=g(\mathfrak{D})\in G
.
Then, for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{0}^{z}.

(0.2.43)

z
z_{1}
z_{s}
\gamma
\mathcal{C}^{+}
\mathcal{C}^{-}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 4:
A path
\gamma
for the proof of
Proposition
0.2.19
.

Proof.

Let
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
be a given general point.
One can find an admissible curve
\gamma

for
\mathfrak{D}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\gamma
intersects
z
.

•

\gamma
has the initial point in

\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
and the final point in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})
.

•

The velocity vector of
\gamma
at any point
satisfies
\langle e_{i},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle<0
for any
i
.

For example, take the straight line passing through
z

from
z+a\sum_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}^{*}\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
to
z-a\sum_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}^{*}\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})

for sufficiently large
a>0
.
Then, slightly deform it to avoid intersecting with
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Consider the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
at
\ell
.
Let
z_{1}
, …,
z_{s}
be the intersections of
\gamma
with walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}

in this order such that
z_{j}=z
.
(If
z
is not on any wall of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
,
we regard it on a wall with the trivial wall element
\mathrm{id}
.)
See Figure
4
.
Thanks to Lemma
0.2.9
,
by slightly deforming
\gamma
if necessary,
we may assume that all
z_{1}
, …,
z_{s}
are general.
The intersection signs
\epsilon_{i}
are all 1
due to the velocity condition.
Then, we have

\displaystyle g\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}=g_{z_{s}}(\mathfrak{D})\cdots g_{z_{j+1}}(\mathfrak{D})g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})g_{z_{j-1}}(\mathfrak{D})\cdots g_{z_{1}}(\mathfrak{D})\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.44)

Let
n_{i}\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
be the
normal vector of the wall crossed by
\gamma
at
z_{i}
.
Then,
we have
\langle n_{i},z\rangle<0

for
i<j
,
and
\langle n_{i},z\rangle>0

for
i>j
.
This implies that

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})\equiv g_{0}^{z}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.45)

Thus, we have
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{0}^{z}
.
∎

The above result, together with Lemma

0.2.11
, shows that a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

is recovered from
a single element
g(\mathfrak{D})
up to equivalence.
In fact, we have the following stronger result.

Theorem 0.2.20

(
[
KS14
, Theorem 2.1.6]
,
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.17]
)
.

The assignment
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:\mathfrak{D}\mapsto g(\mathfrak{D})
gives a one-to-one correspondence between equivalence classes of
consistent scattering diagrams and elements in
G
.

Proof.

Injectivity of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
be consistent scattering diagrams,
and suppose that
g(\mathfrak{D})=g(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})=g
.
Then, by (
0.2.43
),

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime})=g_{0}^{z}

(0.2.46)

holds for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then, by Lemma

0.2.11
,
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
are equivalent.

Surjectivity of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.

Step 1
(Construction of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}:g\mapsto\mathfrak{D}(g)
).
Take
an arbitrary element
g\in G
.
Let
g_{0}^{z}
be the one in
Proposition
0.2.16
.
First, we construct a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}(g)
such that

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{0}^{z}

(0.2.47)

holds for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let us fix any
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.
For each
\ell>0
,
the hyperplane
n^{\perp}
is divided into
finitely many cones of codimension 1
by
n^{\prime}{}^{\perp}

with

n^{\prime}\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
,

n^{\prime}\neq n
.
Then, for general
z\in n^{\perp}
,

\pi_{\ell}(g_{0}^{z})
is constant with respect to
z
on each cone.
Let
\mathfrak{d}
be any of such cones
and
z\in\mathfrak{d}
be any general point.
By (
0.2.37
),
g_{0}^{z}
is uniquely written as

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}=\exp\biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}c_{z,jn}X_{jn}\biggr)\quad(c_{z,jn}\in\mathbbm{k}).

(0.2.48)

Then, with
\mathfrak{d}
,
we associate a wall
(\mathfrak{d},g_{\mathfrak{d}})_{n}
,
where

\displaystyle g_{\mathfrak{d}}=\begin{cases}\exp(c_{z,jn}X_{jn})&\text{$\ell=j\deg(n)$ for some unique integer $j>0$},\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(0.2.49)

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be the collection of all such walls for all

n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
and
\ell
.
Then,
\mathfrak{D}
satisfies
the finiteness condition by construction.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{D}
is a scattering diagram.
Moreover,
for any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})&=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{j>0}\exp(c_{z,jn}X_{jn})&\text{$z\in n^{\perp}$ for some unique $n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}$},\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
&=g_{0}^{z}\end{split}

(0.2.50)

as desired.

Step 2. Let us show that
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent.
Consider the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
at
\ell
.
Let
\gamma
be any admissible curve with the initial point
w

and and the final point
w^{\prime}
.
Let
z_{1}
, …,
z_{s}
be the intersections of
\gamma
with walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}

in this order.
By slightly deforming
\gamma
, if necessary,
we may assume that all
z_{1}
, …,
z_{s}
are general.
Then, by (
0.2.50
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}=(g_{0}^{z_{s}})^{\epsilon_{s}}\cdots(g_{0}^{z_{1}})^{\epsilon_{1}},

(0.2.51)

where
\epsilon_{i}
is the intersection sign at
z_{i}
.
Let
w_{1}=w
,
w_{2}
, …,
w_{s+1}=w^{\prime}
,
where
w_{i}
(
i=2,\dots,s
)
is any general points on
\gamma
between
z_{i-1}
and
z_{i}
.
Note that, when
\epsilon_{i}=1
(resp.
\epsilon_{i}=-1
),

z_{i}
,
w_{i}
,
w_{i+1}
(resp.
z_{i}
,
w_{i+1}
,
w_{i}
)
are in the same configuration as

z
,
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
in Lemma
0.2.17
.
Therefore, by (
0.2.38
) and (
0.2.39
),
we have

\displaystyle(g_{0}^{z_{i}})^{\epsilon_{i}}\equiv(g_{+}^{w_{i+1}})^{-1}g_{+}^{w_{i}}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.52)

Putting it into (
0.2.51
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}\equiv(g_{+}^{w^{\prime}})^{-1}g_{+}^{w}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.2.53)

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=(g_{+}^{w^{\prime}})^{-1}g_{+}^{w},

(0.2.54)

which only depends on the end points
w
and
w^{\prime}
of
\gamma
.

Step 3. Finally, we show that
g(\mathfrak{D})=g
.
Let
\gamma_{+-}
be any admissible curve with the initial point
w
in

\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
and the final point
w^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})
.
Then,
we have the equality (
0.2.54
).
Meanwhile,
for any
n\in N^{+}
, we have

\langle n,w\rangle>0
and
\langle n,w^{\prime}\rangle<0
.
Thus, the decompositions of
g
with respect to
w
and
w^{\prime}

yield
g_{+}^{w}=g
and
g_{+}^{w^{\prime}}=\mathrm{id}
.
Therefore, we have
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{+-},\mathfrak{D}}=g
.
Thus, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is surjective.
∎

Remark 0.2.21
.

Theorem
0.2.20
and its proof only depend on the facts that
\mathfrak{g}

is
N^{+}
-graded and
\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}
is abelian.
Thus, it is applicable to a much wider class of
\mathfrak{g}
.

To summarize the results in this section,
all essential information of any consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is
encoded in the corresponding element
g(\mathfrak{D})=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{+-},\mathfrak{D}}
.
Conversely,
for any element
g
of
G
,
one can construct a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}(g)
,
and admissible curves
\gamma_{+-}
therein
correspond to various factorizations of
g

with
g_{0}^{z}
’s.

Remark 0.2.22
.

The construction of a consistent scattering diagram in
Theorem
0.2.20
also tells that
one may assume that, for the support
\mathfrak{d}\subset n^{\perp}
of any wall,
each face of
\mathfrak{d}
of codimension 2
lies in the intersection with a hyperplane
n^{\prime\perp}

with a
positive
normal vector
n^{\prime}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.

0.2.5
Minimal support

Often it is convenient to consider a consistent
scattering diagram whose support is
minimal

among all equivalent diagrams.

Definition 0.2.23

(Minimal support)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram and
g=g(\mathfrak{D})
be
the corresponding element in
G
.
We say that
\mathfrak{D}
is
with minimal support

if it satisfies the following condition:

•

For any general
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
g_{0}^{z}=\mathrm{id}
,
there is no wall
(\mathfrak{d},g_{\mathfrak{d}})_{n}
of
\mathfrak{D}
such that
z\in\mathfrak{d}
.

It is clear that the support of such
\mathfrak{D}
is the minimal set among the supports of all consistent scattering diagrams
that are equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}
.

Example 0.2.24
.

All examples of rank 2 consistent scattering diagrams in Section
0.2.2
are
with minimal support.

Proposition 0.2.25
.

For any consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, there is
a (not unique) consistent scattering diagram with minimal support
that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
0.2.20
,

\mathfrak{D}
is equivalent to the consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}(g)
for some
g\in G
constructed in the proof
of the surjectivity of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in Theorem
0.2.20
.
Let
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
be a general point such that
g_{0}^{z}=\mathrm{id}
.
Then, for any wall
(\mathfrak{d},g_{\mathfrak{d}})_{n}
in
\mathfrak{D}(g)
such that
z\in\mathfrak{d}
,
we have
g_{\sigma}=\mathrm{id}
by
(
0.2.49
).
Therefore, any such wall is removed form
\mathfrak{D}(g)
up to equivalence.
The resulting diagram has the desired property.
∎

Notes

The contents are taken mostly from
[
GHKK18
, Sect. 1.2]
,

[
KS14
, Sect. 2]

and partly from
[
Bri17
]

with added/modified proofs.
The consistent scattering diagrams here are a special case of
consistent scattering diagrams
in
[
GS11
]
and

wall-crossing structures
(WCS)
in

[
KS14
]
.
The contents in Section
0.2.5
is added by us.

0.3
Cluster scattering diagrams

Here we introduce a special class of consistent scattering diagrams
called
cluster scattering diagrams
(CSDs).
Then, we show the construction/existence of CSDs.
This is the first fundamental result in the application of scattering diagrams
to cluster algebras.

0.3.1
Parametrization of
G
by parallel subgroups

For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
of a (possibly degenerate) fixed data
\Gamma
,
let
\mathfrak{g}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
G
be the ones in Section
0.1.2
.
We introduce another decomposition of
G
.

For any
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,
we introduce the
decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
by
n
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{n}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}),

(0.3.1)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{n}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n^{\prime}\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\{n^{\prime},n\}>0}\mathfrak{g}_{n^{\prime}},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n^{\prime}\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\{n^{\prime},n\}=0}\mathfrak{g}_{n^{\prime}},\quad\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{n}=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n^{\prime}\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\{n^{\prime},n\}<0}\mathfrak{g}_{n^{\prime}}.

(0.3.2)

By (
0.1.18
),

\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{n}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{n}

are Lie subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}^{n}
,

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}^{n}
,

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}^{n}

be their completions,
and let

G_{+}^{n}
,
G_{0}^{n}
,
G_{-}^{n}

be the corresponding subgroups of
G
.

Example 0.3.1
.

In the extreme case of degeneracy
\{\cdot,\cdot\}=0
,

G=G_{0}^{n}

for any
n
.

By Lemma
0.2.15
,
we have the
decomposition of
G
by
n
.

Lemma 0.3.2

(
[
GHKK18
, §1.2]
)
.

For any

n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,
we have

\displaystyle G=G_{+}^{n}G_{0}^{n}G_{-}^{n}

(0.3.3)

so that
any element
g
of
G
is
uniquely factorized as

\displaystyle g=g_{+}^{n}g_{0}^{n}g_{-}^{n}\quad(g_{+}^{n}\in G_{+}^{n},\ g_{0}^{n}\in G_{0}^{n},\ g_{-}^{n}\in G_{-}^{n}).

(0.3.4)

In contrast to the decomposition in
Proposition
0.2.16
,
the above decomposition directly depends on
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,
not each point
z
on
n^{\perp}
.

One can further decompose

\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}=\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}),

(0.3.5)

where
\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}
is
the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}
in (
0.1.31
), and
