\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp}:=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n^{\prime}\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle\{n^{\prime},n\}=0,\,n^{\prime}\not\in\mathbb{Z}n}\mathfrak{g}_{n^{\prime}}.

(0.3.6)

Lemma 0.3.3
.

\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp}
is
a Lie algebra ideal of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n},\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp},

(0.3.7)

or equivalently,
[\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n},\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}]\cap\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}=\{0\}
.
Suppose that
X_{n_{1}},X_{n_{2}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n}

and
[X_{n_{1}},X_{n_{2}}]\in\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}
.
Then, we have

\{n_{i},n\}=0
, and
n_{1}+n_{2}=jn
for some
j>0
.
It follows that
\{n_{1},n_{2}\}=\{n_{1},jn\}=0
.
Thus, by (
0.1.18
),

[X_{n_{1}},X_{n_{2}}]=\{n_{1},n_{2}\}X_{n_{1}+n_{2}}=0
.
∎

Let

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}^{\parallel}
and

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}^{\perp}

be the completions of
\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\parallel}
and

\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp}
,
and let
G_{n}^{\parallel}
and
G_{n}^{\perp}

be the corresponding subgroups of
G
, respectively.

Lemma 0.3.4

(
[
GHKK18
, §1.2]
)
.

For any
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
, the following facts hold.

(a).
G_{n}^{\perp}
is a normal subgroup of
G_{0}^{n}
.

(b).
We have

\displaystyle G_{0}^{n}=G_{n}^{\parallel}G_{n}^{\perp}

(0.3.8)

so that any element
g_{0}^{n}
of
G_{0}^{n}
is
uniquely factorized as

\displaystyle g_{0}^{n}=g_{n}^{\parallel}g_{n}^{\perp}\quad(g_{n}^{\parallel}\in G_{n}^{\parallel},\ g_{n}^{\perp}\in G_{n}^{\perp}).

(0.3.9)

(c).
The map

\displaystyle G_{0}^{n}\rightarrow G_{n}^{\parallel},\quad g_{0}^{n}=g_{n}^{\parallel}g_{n}^{\perp}\mapsto g_{n}^{\parallel}

(0.3.10)

is a group homomorphism with the kernel
G_{n}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(a).
Let
X\in(\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\perp})^{\leq\ell}
and
Y\in(\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{n})^{\leq\ell}
.
Then,
by (
0.1.33
),
the product

\exp(Y)\exp(X)\exp(Y)^{-1}

is rewritten as a product of
\exp(X)
and exponentials of
the commutators and
the higher commutators of
X
and
Y
.
By Lemma
0.3.3
,
it belongs to
(G_{n}^{\perp})^{\leq\ell}
.
(b).
This follows from Lemma
0.2.15
.
(c).
This follows from (a).
∎

Combining Lemmas
0.3.2
and
0.3.4
,
we obtain a map

\displaystyle\psi_{n}:G\rightarrow G_{0}^{n}\rightarrow G_{n}^{\parallel},\quad g\mapsto g_{0}^{n}\mapsto g_{n}^{\parallel}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}).

(0.3.11)

(The map
\psi_{n}
is not a group homomorphism except for
n=e_{i}
.
See Lemma
0.3.17
.)
Consider a set (not a group)

\displaystyle S^{\parallel}=S^{\parallel}_{G}:=\prod_{n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}}G_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.12)

We then have a map (not a group homomorphism),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}:G\rightarrow S^{\parallel},\quad g\mapsto\mathbf{g}^{\parallel}=(g_{n}^{\parallel})_{n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}}.

(0.3.13)

We note that

\displaystyle(G_{n}^{\parallel})^{\leq\ell}=\{\mathrm{id}\}\quad(n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{>\ell}).

(0.3.14)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}(g)=\mathrm{id}\quad(g\in G_{n}^{\parallel},\ n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{>\ell}).

(0.3.15)

Theorem 0.3.5

(
[
GHKK18
, Prop. 1.20]
)
.

The map
\boldsymbol{\psi}:G\rightarrow S^{\parallel}
is a bijection.

Our proof is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 0.3.6

(cf.
[
GHKK18
, Appendix C.1]
)
.

(a).
For the projection
\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}:G^{\leq\ell+1}\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell}
in (
0.1.26
),

\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}
is in the center of
G^{\leq\ell+1}
,
and it is isomorphic (as a group) to the direct product of groups,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}\simeq\prod_{n\in(N^{+})^{\ell+1}}G_{n},\quad G_{n}=\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{n}),

(0.3.16)

where the isomorphism is given by
\pi_{\ell+1}(\prod_{n}g_{n})\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}}(g_{n})
.

(b).
G^{\leq 1}

is an abelian group, and it is isomorphic (as a group) to the direct product
of groups,

\displaystyle G^{\leq 1}\simeq\prod_{n\in(N^{+})^{\leq 1}}G_{n},

(0.3.17)

where the isomorphism is given by
\pi_{1}(\prod_{n}g_{n})\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}}(g_{n})
.

Proof.

We work with the corresponding Lie algebras.
Let
\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}:\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell+1}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
be the
canonical projection.
Then, as a vector space, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\in(N^{+})^{\ell+1}}\mathfrak{g}_{n}.

(0.3.18)

Moreover,

\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}
is in the center of
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell+1}
by
(
0.1.18
).
Therefore, the isomorphism (
0.3.18
) holds also as a Lie algebra,
where the right hand side is viewed as the direct sum of Lie algebras.
The proof of (b) is similar.
∎

Proof of Theorem
0.3.5
.

Surjectivity of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

Let
\mathbf{h}=(h_{n})_{n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}}
be a given element in
S^{\parallel}
.
Let us construct an element
g\in G

inductively with respect to degree
\ell

such that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(g)=\mathbf{h}
,
that is,

g_{n}^{\parallel}=h_{n}
(
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
) holds.
To show it, we recursively construct elements
g_{\ell}\in G
(
\ell=1,2,\dots
)
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}((g_{\ell})_{n}^{\parallel})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}(h_{n})\quad(n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}),

(0.3.19)

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}(g_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}(g_{\ell}).

(0.3.20)

By (
0.3.20
),
the limit
g=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}g_{\ell}\in G
is well-defined.
Moreover, by (
0.3.19
),
g
has the desired property.

Step 1. We first consider the case
\ell=1
.
We define

\displaystyle g_{1}=\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq 1}}h_{n}\in G,

(0.3.21)

where the order of the (finite) product is arbitrarily chosen,
and
the element
g_{1}
depends on the order of the product.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(g_{1})=\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq 1}}\pi_{1}(h_{n}),

(0.3.22)

where factors
\pi_{1}(h_{n})
commute with each other,
because
G^{\leq 1}
is abelian by Lemma
0.3.6
(b).
It follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}((g_{1})_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{1}(h_{n})\quad(n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq 1}).

(0.3.23)

Step 2.
Next, for some
\ell>0
, suppose that we have
g_{\ell}\in G

such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}((g_{\ell})_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{\ell}(h_{n})\quad(n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell})

(0.3.24)

holds.
For any
n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell+1}
,
let
h^{\prime}_{n}\in G_{n}^{\parallel}

be the one such that

\displaystyle(g_{\ell})_{n}^{\parallel}h^{\prime}_{n}=h_{n}.

(0.3.25)

We note that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}(h^{\prime}_{n})\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}.

(0.3.26)

This is true for
n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}

by (
0.3.24
),
and for
n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\ell+1}

by (
0.3.15
).
Now, we define

\displaystyle g_{\ell+1}=g_{\ell}\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell+1}}h^{\prime}_{n},

(0.3.27)

where the order of the (finite) product is arbitrarily chosen,
and
the element
g_{\ell+1}
depends on the order of the product.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}(g_{\ell+1})=\pi_{\ell+1}(g_{\ell})\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell+1}}\pi_{\ell+1}(h^{\prime}_{n}).

(0.3.28)

By (
0.3.26
) and Lemma
0.3.6
(a),
\pi_{\ell+1}(h^{\prime}_{n})
commutes with
any elements in
G^{\leq\ell+1}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}((g_{\ell+1})_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{\ell+1}((g_{\ell})_{n}^{\parallel})\pi_{\ell+1}(h^{\prime}_{n})=\pi_{\ell+1}(h_{n})\quad(n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell+1}).

(0.3.29)

Moreover, by (
0.3.26
) and (
0.3.28
),
we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}(g_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}(g_{\ell}).

(0.3.30)

Injectivity of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

For given
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, assume that

\boldsymbol{\psi}(g)=\boldsymbol{\psi}(g^{\prime})=\mathbf{g}^{\parallel}
.
Let us show that
g=g^{\prime}
; namely, we have,
for any
\ell>0
,

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}(g)=\pi_{\ell}(g^{\prime}).

(0.3.31)

By Lemma
0.3.6
(b) and the assumption, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(g)=\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq 1}}\pi_{1}(g_{n}^{\parallel})=\prod_{n\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq 1}}\pi_{1}(g^{\prime}{}_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{1}(g^{\prime}).

(0.3.32)

Thus, the equality (
0.3.31
) holds for
\ell=1
.
Suppose that the equality holds for some
\ell
.
Then,
\pi_{\ell+1}(g)\pi_{\ell+1}(g^{\prime})^{-1}\in\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}
.
So, by Lemma
0.3.6
(a),
there are some
h_{n}\in G_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}(g)=\pi_{\ell+1}(g^{\prime})\prod_{n\in(N^{+})^{\ell+1}}\pi_{\ell+1}(h_{n}).

(0.3.33)

Since

\pi_{\ell+1}(h_{n})
are central elements in
G^{\leq\ell+1}
,
for
n\in(N^{+})_{\mathrm{pr}}^{\leq\ell}

and a positive integer
t
such that

tn\in(N^{+})^{\ell+1}
,
we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}(g_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{\ell+1}(g^{\prime}{}_{n}^{\parallel})\pi_{\ell+1}(h_{tn}),

(0.3.34)

Meanwhile,
by the assumption,
we have

g_{n}^{\parallel}=(g^{\prime})_{n}^{\parallel}
.
Therefore,

\pi_{\ell+1}(g_{n}^{\parallel})=\pi_{\ell+1}((g^{\prime})_{n}^{\parallel})
.
It follows that
\pi_{\ell+1}(h_{n})=\mathrm{id}

for any
n\in(N^{+})^{\ell+1}
.
Putting it back in (
0.3.33
), we conclude that
\pi_{\ell+1}(g)=\pi_{\ell+1}(g^{\prime})
.
∎

0.3.2
Incoming and outgoing walls

Let

\mathbf{h}=(h_{n})_{n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}}

be an arbitrary element of
the set

S^{\parallel}
in (
0.3.12
).
The bijection
\boldsymbol{\psi}
in Theorem
0.3.5

uniquely determines the element
g(\mathbf{h})\in G

such that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(g(\mathbf{h}))=\mathbf{h}
.
In turn,
the bijection
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in Theorem
0.2.20

uniquely determines a consistent scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}(g(\mathbf{h}))
up to equivalence
such that
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\mathfrak{D}(g(\mathbf{h})))=g(\mathbf{h})
.
Let us write
\mathfrak{D}(g(\mathbf{h}))
as
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
for simplicity.
Below we will give an alternative description
of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
directly by
\mathbf{h}
.

Let
p^{*}
be the map in (
0.1.8
).
For
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle p^{*}(n)\in n^{\perp},

(0.3.35)

because
\langle n,p^{*}(n)\rangle=\{n,n\}=0
.

Remark 0.3.7
.

When
\Gamma
is degenerate,
p^{*}(n)
is not injective.
However, this fact is irrelevant to all results in Section
0.3
.

For any
g\in G
,
we have the following relation between

g_{0}^{z}
(
z\in n^{\perp}
) and
g_{n}^{\parallel}

around
p^{*}(n)
.

Lemma 0.3.8

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.21]
)
.

Let
g\in G
and
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.
Then, for any
\ell>0
,
there is an open neighborhood
U_{\ell}\subset n^{\perp}
of
p^{*}(n)

such that

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}\equiv g_{n}^{\parallel}\mod G^{>\ell}\quad(z\in U_{\ell},\ \text{$z$: general}\,).

(0.3.36)

In other words,

\displaystyle\lim_{\scriptstyle z\rightarrow p^{*}(n)\atop\scriptstyle z\in n^{\perp},\,z:\,\rm{general}}g_{0}^{z}=g_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.37)

Proof.

Case 1
. Suppose that
p^{*}(n)
is general.
This occurs if and only if
the fixed data
\Gamma
is of rank 2 and nondegenerate.
Thus, any hyperplane
n^{\perp}
intersects
other hyperplane
n^{\prime}{}^{\perp}

with

n^{\prime}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,

n^{\prime}\neq n

only at the origin.
Then,
n^{\perp}
is divided into two rays,
on each of which
g_{0}^{z}
is constant.
It follows that,
if a general
z\in n^{\perp}
and
p^{*}(n)\neq 0
belong to
the same ray,

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}=g_{0}^{p^{*}(n)}

(0.3.38)

holds.
Therefore, it is enough to prove

\displaystyle g_{0}^{p^{*}(n)}=g_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.39)

By comparing (
0.2.29
) and (
0.3.1
),
we see that
two decompositions

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{+}^{p^{*}(n)}g_{0}^{p^{*}(n)}g_{-}^{p^{*}(n)},

(0.3.40)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{+}^{n}g_{0}^{n}g_{-}^{n}

(0.3.41)

coincide.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle g_{0}^{n}=g_{0}^{p^{*}(n)}\in G_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.42)

This further implies that

\displaystyle g_{0}^{n}=g_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.43)

Thus, we have the equality (
0.3.39
).

n^{\perp}
p^{*}(n)
z
\mathcal{C}^{+}
\mathcal{C}^{-}
\underbrace{\mbox{\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt}}_{\displaystyle U_{\ell}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 5:
Configuration
for Case 2 of the proof of Lemma
0.3.8
.

Case 2
.
Suppose that
p^{*}(n)
is not general.
Fix an integer
\ell>0
.
Then, there might be some (possibly no)
n^{\prime}\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
,

n^{\prime}\neq n

such that
\langle n^{\prime},p^{*}(n)\rangle=\{n^{\prime},n\}=0
.
We choose an open neighborhood of

U_{\ell}\subset n^{\perp}

of

p^{*}(n)
that does not intersect any
n^{\prime\prime}{}^{\perp}
(
n^{\prime\prime}\in(N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+})^{\leq\ell}
) such that

\langle n^{\prime\prime},p^{*}(n)\rangle=\{n^{\prime\prime},n\}\neq 0
.
See Figure
5
.
Consider the decomposition of
g
by
n

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=g_{+}^{n}g_{0}^{n}g_{-}^{n}.

(0.3.44)

We further decompose
g_{0}^{n}
at general
z\in U_{\ell}
,

\displaystyle g_{0}^{n}=h_{+}^{z}h_{0}^{z}h_{-}^{z}\quad(h_{+}^{z}\in G_{+}^{z},\ h_{0}^{z}\in G_{0}^{z}=G_{n}^{\parallel},\ h_{-}^{z}\in G_{-}^{z}).

(0.3.45)

Then,
h_{\pm}^{z}\in G_{n}^{\perp}
and
h_{0}^{z}\in G_{n}^{\parallel}
.
Therefore, by applying the group homomorphism
in (
0.3.10
) to (
0.3.45
),
we obtain

\displaystyle h_{0}^{z}=g_{n}^{\parallel}.

(0.3.46)

On the other hand, by the assumption on
U_{\ell}
,
we have
\pi_{\ell}(g_{\pm}^{n})\in(G_{\pm}^{z})^{\leq\ell}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=(g_{+}^{n}h_{+}^{z})h_{0}^{z}(h_{-}^{z}g_{-}^{n})

(0.3.47)

is regarded as the decomposition of
g
at
z
modulo
G^{>\ell}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle h_{0}^{z}\equiv g_{0}^{z}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.3.48)

Then, by (
0.3.46
) and (
0.3.48
), we obtain the equality (
0.3.36
).
∎

Definition 0.3.9
.

A wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{n}

is
incoming
(
resp. outgoing
) if

\displaystyle p^{*}(n)\in\mathfrak{d}

(0.3.49)

holds (resp. otherwise)
for its normal vector
n
.

Now we come back to our problem.
For any
\mathbf{h}\in S^{\parallel}
,
we define a set of incoming walls,

\displaystyle\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h}):=\{(n^{\perp},h_{n})_{n}\mid n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}\}.

(0.3.50)

For a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}

denote the set of all incoming walls of
\mathfrak{D}
.

The following theorem characterizes
a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})

corresponding to
\mathbf{h}\in S^{\parallel}
up to equivalence.

Theorem 0.3.10

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.21]
)
.

For any
\mathbf{h}\in S^{\parallel}
,
let
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
be a corresponding consistent scattering diagram
up to equivalence.
Then, the following facts hold.

(a). There is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h})
such that

\mathfrak{D}
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
.

(b).
Any consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h})

is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
.

Proof.

(a).
As a representative of
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
,
we choose
\mathfrak{D}(g)

in
Step 1 in the proof of the surjectivity of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

in Theorem
0.2.20

with
g=g(\mathbf{h})
.
For each
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
, up to the equivalence we rearrange
the walls of
\mathfrak{D}(g)
in
n^{\perp}

to have the desired property as follows.
For each integer
\ell>0
,
the hyperplane
n^{\perp}
is divided into
cones, say,
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
, …,
\mathfrak{d}_{p}

such that
\pi_{\ell}(g_{0}^{z})
is constant
with respect to general
z
on each cone.
Let us write the associated walls therein
as
(\mathfrak{d}_{1},g_{1})_{n}
, …,
(\mathfrak{d}_{p},g_{p})_{n}
.
Let
U_{\ell}
be the open neighborhood of
p^{*}(n)
in
n^{\perp}
in
Lemma
0.3.8
.
When
p^{*}(n)
is in the interior of some cone, say,
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
,
we may assume that

p^{*}(n)\in\mathfrak{d}_{1}

and
p^{*}(n)\not\in\mathfrak{d}_{i}
(
i\neq 1
).
When
p^{*}(n)
is on the boundary of some cone,
there are several cones that
intersect
U_{\ell}
.
By the property (
0.3.36
),

\pi_{\ell}(g_{0}^{z})
is constant on these cones so that

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}(g_{0}^{z})\equiv g_{n}^{\parallel}=h_{n}\quad\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.3.51)

Therefore, the attached wall element in (
0.2.49
)
is also constant.
Thus, one can join these walls into a single wall.
After renumbering cones,
we may assume that
p^{*}(n)\in\mathfrak{d}_{1}

and
p^{*}(n)\not\in\mathfrak{d}_{i}
(
i\neq 1
).
Then, we do the following:

•

Remove the incoming wall
(\mathfrak{d}_{1},g_{1})_{n}
.

•

Replace the outgoing walls
(\mathfrak{d}_{i},g_{i})_{n}
with
(\mathfrak{d}_{i},g_{i}g_{1}^{-1})_{n}

for
i\neq 1
.

We do this for all
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
and
\ell>0
,
so that all walls
that are left
are outgoing.
Then, finally, we add walls of
\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h})
.
The resulting scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{h})
,
and it has the desired property.

(b).
Suppose that
\mathfrak{D}
is a consistent scattering diagram
with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h})
.
Let
g=g(\mathfrak{D})
.
By Theorems

0.2.20
and
0.3.5
, it is enough to prove

\displaystyle g_{n}^{\parallel}=h_{n}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}).

(0.3.52)

Let us fix
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.
There is an incoming wall
\mathbf{w}_{n}=(n^{\perp},h_{n})_{n}
,
and all other walls with the normal vector
n
do not contain
p^{*}(n)
.
Then, for each integer
\ell>0
,
there is a neighborhood
V_{\ell}
of
p^{*}(n)
in
n^{\perp}

that does not intersect any outgoing walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}

with the normal vector
n
.
Thus, for any general
z\in V_{\ell}
,

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}=g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})\equiv h_{n}\mod G^{>\ell}.

(0.3.53)

It follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\scriptstyle z\rightarrow p^{*}(n)\atop\scriptstyle z\in n^{\perp},\ z:\,\rm{general}}g_{0}^{z}=h_{n}.

(0.3.54)

Meanwhile,
by Lemma
0.3.8
,
the left hand side coincides with

g_{n}^{\parallel}
.
Thus, the equality (
0.3.52
) holds.
∎

0.3.3
Cluster scattering diagrams

Now we are ready to introduce a special class of consistent scattering diagrams,
which is the main subject of Part III.

Definition 0.3.11

(Cluster scattering diagram (CSD))
.

For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
,
we define a set of incoming walls

\displaystyle\mathrm{In}_{\mathfrak{s}}:=

\displaystyle\ \{\mathbf{w}_{e_{i}}:=(e_{i}^{\perp},\Psi[e_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e_{i}}\mid i=1,\dots,r\}.

(0.3.55)

Then, a
cluster scattering diagram
(CSD for short)
for
\mathfrak{s}

is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

with
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Note that the wall elements in (
0.3.55
)
is in the normalized form in (
0.1.73
)
so that this definition is invariant under the rescaling
of the fixed data
\Gamma
.

The following theorem is the first fundamental result on CSDs.

Theorem 0.3.12

(Existence/uniqueness of CSD
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.21]
)
.

For any seed
\mathfrak{s}
, a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for
\mathfrak{s}
uniquely exists
up to equivalence.

Proof.

For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
, let
\mathbf{h}_{\mathfrak{s}}=(h_{\mathfrak{s},n})\in S^{\parallel}

be the one with

\displaystyle h_{\mathfrak{s},n}=\begin{cases}\Psi[e_{i}]^{\delta_{i}}&n=e_{i}\ (i=1,\dots,r),\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{other $n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}.$}\end{cases}

(0.3.56)

Then, by Theorem
0.3.10
,
there is a unique consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}(\mathbf{h}_{\mathfrak{s}})

up to equivalence.
Then, by removing all trivial incoming walls
(n^{\perp},\mathrm{id})_{n}

(
n\neq e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
),
we obtain
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
∎

Definition 0.3.13

(Cluster element)
.

For the above
\mathbf{h}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
the corresponding element
g_{\mathfrak{s}}:=g(\mathbf{h}_{\mathfrak{s}})\in G

is called the
cluster element
for a seed
\mathfrak{s}
.

Example 0.3.14
.

All examples of consistent scattering diagrams
in Section
0.2.2
are CSDs
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

with the cluster element
g_{\mathfrak{s}}=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}
.
By (
0.1.10
), we have

\displaystyle p^{*}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{ij}f_{i}.

(0.3.57)

Thus, in the identification
N\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
therein,
p^{*}(\mathbf{n})=B\mathbf{n}=(-\delta_{1}n_{1},\delta_{2}n_{2})
,
which is in the second quadrant of
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It follows that the rays in the fourth quadrant are all outgoing walls.
They are of
finite type
, that is, one can choose a representative of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

with only finitely many walls.
In fact, they are the only rank 2 CSDs
of finite type
up to equivalence.

0.3.4
Constancy of total wall-element on
e_{i}^{\perp}

Here we present some property of walls with the normal vector
e_{i}

for any consistent scattering diagram.
For this purpose, we introduce yet another decomposition of
G
.

For each
e_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,r
),
we introduce the
decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
by
e_{i}
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel},

(0.3.58)

where
\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}

is a Lie subalgebra
of
\mathfrak{g}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}:=\bigoplus_{\scriptstyle n\in N^{+}\atop\scriptstyle n\notin\mathbb{Z}e_{i}}\mathfrak{g}_{n}=\mathfrak{g}_{+}^{e_{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-}^{e_{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\perp}.

(0.3.59)

Lemma 0.3.15
.

\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}
is
a Lie algebra ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

This follows from the following simple fact:
For
n,n^{\prime}\in N^{+}
, we have
n+n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}e_{i}
if and only if
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}e_{i}
.
∎

Remark 0.3.16
.

On the contrary, for
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}

other than
e_{1}
, …,
e_{r}
,
the Lie subalgebra

\mathfrak{g}_{n}^{\nparallel}
defined similarly
is not a Lie algebra ideal of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Let

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}

be the completion of
\mathfrak{g}_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}
,
and let
G_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}
be the corresponding subgroup of
G
.
We have
the
decomposition of
G
by
e_{i}
and
a parallel result to
Lemma
0.3.4
.

Lemma 0.3.17
.

The following facts hold.

(a).
G_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}
is a normal subgroup of
G
.

(b).
We have

\displaystyle G=G_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}G_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}

(0.3.60)

so that
any element
g
of
G
is
uniquely factorized as

\displaystyle g=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}g_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}\quad(g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}\in G_{e_{i}}^{\parallel},\ g_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}\in G_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}).

(0.3.61)

(c).
The map

\displaystyle\chi_{e_{i}}:G\rightarrow G_{e_{i}}^{\parallel},\quad g=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}g_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}\mapsto g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}

(0.3.62)

is a group homomorphism with the kernel
G_{e_{i}}^{\nparallel}
.

(d). The map
\chi_{e_{i}}
in (
0.3.62
)
coincides with the map
\psi_{e_{i}}
in (
0.3.11
).

Proof.

The proofs of (a)–(c) are the same as Lemma
0.3.4
.
Let us prove (d).
Let
g=g_{+}^{e_{i}}g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}g_{e_{i}}^{\perp}g_{-}^{e_{i}}

be the decomposition in Lemmas
0.3.2

and
0.3.4
.
By applying the map
\chi_{e_{i}}
to this equality, we
obtain

\chi_{e_{i}}(g)=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}=\psi_{e_{i}}(g)
.
∎

We have the following consequence of Lemma
0.3.17
.

Proposition 0.3.18
.

(a).
Let
g
be any element of
G
.
Then,
for any general
z\in e_{i}^{\perp}
,
the equality

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}

(0.3.63)

holds. In particular,
g_{0}^{z}
is constant with respect to general
z\in e_{i}^{\perp}
.

(b).
Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any consistent scattering diagram,
and let
g=g(\mathfrak{D})
be the corresponding element in
G
.
Then,
for any general
z\in e_{i}^{\perp}
,
the equality

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D})=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}

(0.3.64)

holds.
In particular,
g_{z}({\mathfrak{D}})
is constant with respect to general
z\in e_{i}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(a).
Let
g
be any element of
G
.
For any general
z\in e_{i}^{\perp}
,
we have the decomposition of
g
at
z
in (
0.2.34
),

\displaystyle g=g_{+}^{z}g_{0}^{z}g_{-}^{z}\quad(g_{+}^{z}\in G_{+}^{z},\ g_{0}^{z}\in G_{e_{i}}^{\parallel},\ g_{-}^{z}\in G_{-}^{z}).

(0.3.65)

Then, applying the map
\chi_{e_{i}}
to this equality, we
obtain

\chi_{e_{i}}(g)=g_{0}^{z}
.
Therefore,
g_{0}^{z}=g_{e_{i}}^{\parallel}
.

(b). This follows from (a) and Proposition
0.2.19
.
∎

In particular, we obtain the following property of a CSD,
which will be used later.

Proposition 0.3.19

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorems 1.28, Remark 1.29]
)
.

There is a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

such that the normal vector of any outgoing wall is other than
e_{1}
, …,
e_{r}
.

Proof.

Take any CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
The total wall element
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
on
e_{i}^{\perp}

of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is constant by Proposition
0.3.18
.
Since the wall
\mathbf{w}_{e_{i}}
in (
0.3.55
) is the only incoming wall
with the normal vector
e_{i}
,

g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
on
e_{i}^{\perp}
coincides with
\Psi[e_{i}]^{\delta_{i}}

by Lemma
0.3.8
.
This means that
the total contribution to
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
on
e_{i}^{\perp}

from all outgoing walls is trivial.
Therefore, one can remove all outgoing walls whose normal vectors
are
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
up to equivalence.
∎

0.3.5
Rank 2 examples: infinite type

We have already seen
all rank 2 CSDs of finite type
in Section
0.2.2
.
Here we consider rank 2 CSDs of infinite type,
where
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\geq 4
for
B
in (
0.2.16
).

Affine type.

The case
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}=4
is exceptional, and it is said to be
of affine type
.
There are two cases.

(a)
A_{1}^{(1)}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
(b)
A_{2}^{(2)}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
(c) non-affine type

Figure 6:
Rank 2 CSD: infinite type

(a). Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
. Let
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=2
.
Note that, for any primitive
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
,

\delta(n)=2
.
The following description of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is well known
[
Rei09
,
GPS10
,
Rea20a
]
:
The walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
are given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(0.3.66)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-p-1),\Psi[(p+1,p)]^{2})_{(p+1,p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.67)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p+1,-p),\Psi[(p,p+1)]^{2})_{(p,p+1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.68)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\Psi[2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{2^{2-j}})_{\mathbf{n}_{0}},

(0.3.69)

where
\mathbf{n}_{0}=(1,1)
.
Note that
\delta(2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0})=2^{1-j}

by (
0.1.68
).
See Figure
6
(a).

Here we demonstrate to derive the above walls, up to some degree, by repeatedly applying the pentagon relation
(
0.1.56
)
in a similar manner
for finite type.
We use the same notation
[n_{1},n_{2}]^{c}

for
\Psi[(n_{1},n_{2})]^{c}

as in Section
0.2.2
.
We apply the pentagon relation
to the cluster element
g_{\mathfrak{s}}=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{2}

repeatedly
and
obtain
the equality

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(0.3.70)

At this moment, the expression in the right hand side
is not yet “ordered” to be presented by
a scattering diagram.
Here, we say that
the above product is
ordered
(resp.
anti-ordered
)
if, for any adjacent pair
[\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}[\mathbf{n}]^{c}
,

\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}=n^{\prime}_{2}n_{1}-n^{\prime}_{1}n_{2}\leq 0
(resp.
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}\geq 0
) holds.
Equivalently,
if we view
[n_{1},n_{2}]
as a fraction

n_{1}/n_{2}
,
then, the numbers should be aligned in the decreasing order
(resp. increasing order)
form left to right.
The left hand side of (
0.3.70
) is anti-ordered.
To make the right hand side of (
0.3.70
) ordered, we need to interchange

[1,2]
and
[1,0]
in the middle,
where

\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}=2
for
\mathbf{n}^{\prime}=(1,2)
and
\mathbf{n}=(1,0)
.
As the lowest approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>3}
.
Then,
[1,2]
and
[1,0]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>3}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(0.3.71)

This certainly agrees with
the data in (
0.3.66
)–(
0.3.69
).
This is also compatible with the formula (
0.1.75
).

To proceed to higher degree, we
now apply the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
)
with
c=2
to the pair
[1,2]^{1/2}
and
[1,0]^{1/2}

in (
0.3.70
),
which are obtained by factorizing the elements
[1,2]
and
[1,0]
.
To clarify the structure among fractional powers,
we use the notation
[n_{1},n_{2}]_{2}:=[n_{1},n_{2}]^{1/2}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}.

(0.3.72)

This is parallel to (
0.3.70
).
As the next approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>7}
.
Then,
[3,4]
and
[1,0]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>7}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}.

(0.3.73)

Then, we plug it into
(
0.3.70
)
and apply the pentagon relation,
and
we have,
modulo
G^{>7}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(0.3.74)

Again,
this agrees with
the data in (
0.3.66
)–(
0.3.69
).
By repeating this procedure modulo
G^{>2^{\ell}-1}
,
we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\cdots\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}^{2^{2-j}}\cdots\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2},

(0.3.75)

which
is equivalent to the result (
0.3.66
)–(
0.3.69
).
A complete proof of the equality (
0.3.75
)
based on the pentagon relation is found in

[
Mat21
]
.

(b). Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
. Let
\delta_{1}=1
,
\delta_{2}=4
.
The situation is parallel to
A_{1}^{(1)}
though a little more complicated.
So, we present results with less explanation.
The following description of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is known
[
Rea20a
]
:
The walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
are given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{4})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(0.3.76)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-2p-1),\Psi[(2p+1,4p)])_{(2p+1,4p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.77)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2p-1,-4p),\Psi[(p,2p-1)]^{4})_{(p,2p-1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.78)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-2p+1),\Psi[(2p-1,4p)])_{(2p-1,4p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.79)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2p+1,-4p),\Psi[(p,2p+1)]^{4})_{(p,2p+1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(0.3.80)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ (\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{6}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\Psi[2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{2^{2-j}})_{\mathbf{n}_{0}},\end{split}

(0.3.81)

where
\mathbf{n}_{0}=(1,2)
.
Note that
\delta(2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0})=2^{1-j}
.
See Figure
6
(b).

We apply the pentagon relation
to the cluster element
g_{\mathfrak{s}}=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{4}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]

repeatedly
and
obtain
the equality

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}.

(0.3.82)

Again, this
is not yet ordered,
and
to order it, we need to interchange

[1,3]
and
[1,1]
in the middle.

As the lowest approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>5}
.
Then,
[1,3]
and
[1,1]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>5}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{6}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}.

(0.3.83)

This certainly agrees with
the data in (
0.3.76
)–(
0.3.81
).

To proceed to higher degree,
we do
in the same way as before.
By apply the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=2
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2},

(0.3.84)

which has a common structure with
(
0.3.70
).
As the next approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>11}
.
Then,
[3,7]
and
[1,1]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>11}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}.

(0.3.85)

Then, we plug it into
(
0.3.82
)
and apply the pentagon relation,
and
we have,
modulo
G^{>11}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{6}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{2}\\
&\qquad\times\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
8\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}.\end{split}

(0.3.86)

Again,
this agrees with
the data in (
0.3.76
)–(
0.3.81
).
By repeating this procedure modulo
G^{>3\cdot 2^{\ell}-1}
,
we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
8\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\cdots\\
&\hskip 60.0pt\times\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{6}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j+1}\end{bmatrix}^{2^{2-j}}\cdots\begin{bmatrix}5\\
12\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
8\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4},\end{split}

(0.3.87)

which is equivalent to the result (
0.3.66
)–(
0.3.69
).
A complete proof of the equality (
0.3.87
)
based on the pentagon relation is found in

[
Mat21
]
.
The case
\delta_{1}=4
,
\delta_{2}=1
is similar.

Non-affine type.

The case
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}>4
.
The entire structure of a CSD is not yet known.
Let

\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})
be the irrational
cone spanned
by two
vectors

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\pm\sqrt{\delta_{1}\delta_{2}(\delta_{1}\delta_{2}-4)}\\
-2\delta_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(0.3.88)

Under the canonical pairing (
0.2.12
),
an outgoing wall
\mathbf{w}
is in the region
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})

if and only if its normal vector
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})
satisfies
the condition

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\delta_{2}+\sqrt{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-4/\delta_{1})}}<\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}<\frac{2}{\delta_{2}-\sqrt{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-4/\delta_{1})}}.

(0.3.89)

See Figure
6
(c),
where the cone
\sigma(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
is depicted as a hatched region.
The region outside
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})

is decomposed into infinitely many chambers
by the walls converging
to
\mathbf{v}_{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}

as in the affine case.
They are the
G
-chambers

in Section
0.6.6
,
and its behavior is well-known in cluster algebra theory
(e.g.,
[
Rea20a
, §3]
).
On the other hand,
in the region
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})
,
which is informally called the
Badlands
,
it is expected that every ray with the normal vector

n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}

in the cone
\sigma(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
appears
as a nontrivial wall
[
GHKK18
, Example 1.15]
.
For the skew-symmetric case
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}
,
this is proved in
[
DM21
, Example 7.10]
.
However, the wall elements are not known yet.
In Section
0.5.12
,
we present results calculated by computer,
which support the above expectation.

Notes

Most contents are taken from
[
GHKK18
, §1.2]

with added/modified proofs.
In particular,
an alternative and simple proof of
Proposition

0.3.19

is given
based on
Proposition
0.3.18
.
The presentation of rank 2 CSDs of affine type
based on the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) is also due to us.
The procedure demonstrated above is
the implementation of the
perturbation trick
and
the
change of lattice trick

described in
[
GHKK18
, Appendix C.3]
,
and it appears later as Algorithm
0.5.7

in more generality.

0.4
Principal extension method

In this short section we give a faithful representation of
the structure group
G
for any (possibly degenerate) fixed data
based on the principal extension method.

0.4.1
Principal extension of fixed data

In the previous sections we have successfully constructed
a CSD for any (possibly degenerate) fixed data.
On the other hand, to study further properties of CSDs,
there is a situation where
a faithful representation of
G
is desired even
for
degenerate
fixed data.
In cluster algebra theory,
this corresponds to the situation where
the algebraically independence of
\hat{y}
-variables
in
[
FZ07
]

is desirable.
To resolve it, the
principal coefficients
,
or equivalently, the
principal extensions
of exchange matrices were introduced in
[
FZ07
]
.
Here we present a parallel method
in scattering diagrams by
[
GHK15
, Construction 2.1]
,
which in particular provides a faithful representation of
G

for any (possibly degenerate) fixed data.

Definition 0.4.1

(Principal extension of fixed data)
.

Let
\Gamma=\{N,\{\cdot,\cdot\},N^{\circ},\allowbreak(\delta_{i}),\allowbreak M,M^{\circ}\}

be a (possibly degenerate) fixed data of rank
r
.
The
principal extension
\Gamma_{\mathrm{prin}}
of
\Gamma

is a fixed data
of rank
2r
consisting of the following data:

•

A lattice
\tilde{N}=N\oplus M^{\circ}
of rank
2r
with
a skew-symmetric bilinear form

\displaystyle\{\cdot,\cdot\}:\tilde{N}\times\tilde{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q},

(0.4.1)

\displaystyle\{(n,m),(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})\}:=\{n,n^{\prime}\}+\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle-\langle n,m^{\prime}\rangle.

(0.4.2)

•

A sublattice
\tilde{N}^{\circ}=N^{\circ}\oplus M\subset\tilde{N}
.
It is of finite index and

\displaystyle\{\tilde{N}^{\circ},\tilde{N}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}.

(0.4.3)

•

A
2r
-tuple of
positive integers
(\tilde{\delta}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\delta}_{2r})=(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r},\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})
.

•

\tilde{M}=\mathrm{Hom}(\tilde{N},\mathbb{Z})\simeq M\oplus N^{\circ}
,

\tilde{M}^{\circ}=\mathrm{Hom}(\tilde{N}^{\circ},\mathbb{Z})\simeq M^{\circ}\oplus N
.

The bilinear form (
0.4.2
) is always nondegenerate,
so that

\Gamma_{\mathrm{prin}}
is an nondegenerate fixed data.

The canonical pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\tilde{N}^{\circ}\times\tilde{M}^{\circ}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is given by

\displaystyle\langle(n,m),(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})\rangle=\langle n,m^{\prime}\rangle+\langle n^{\prime},m\rangle.

(0.4.4)

Example 0.4.2
.

For any seed
\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
for
\Gamma
, let
(f_{1},\dots,f_{r})
be
the basis of
M^{\circ}
in Section
0.1.1
.
Then,
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}=((e_{1},0),\dots,(e_{r},0),(0,f_{1}),\dots,(0,f_{r}))
is a seed
for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{prin}}
.
Also,
((0,f_{1}),\dots,(0,f_{r}),(e_{1},0),\dots,(e_{r},0))
is a basis of

\tilde{M}^{\circ}
.

Remark 0.4.3
.

In the above example,
by applying the correspondence in (
0.1.7
),
and
using
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
in (
0.1.7
),
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{r}).

(0.4.5)

They are regarded as
x
-variables
x_{i}
with
principal coefficients

y_{i}

in
[
FZ07
]
.

0.4.2
Principal
x
-representation of
G

To clarify the background,
let us briefly describe
the scheme of the construction of a CSD in
[
GHKK18
]

for degenerate
\Gamma
.
For a seed
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{prin}}
in Example
0.4.2
, every result in the previous
sections are applicable,
where the structure group for scattering diagrams
is the group
\tilde{G}
associated with
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}
,
and the ambient space
for their supports is
\tilde{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2r}
.
Moreover, since
\Gamma_{\mathrm{prin}}
is nondegenerate,

\tilde{G}
has the faithful
x
-representation.
Then, one can construct a CSD for
\mathfrak{s}
by reducing the structure group
\tilde{G}
to
the subgroup
G
associated with
\mathfrak{s}
.
Moreover,
G
has a faithful representation
induced from the faithful
x
-representation of
\tilde{G}
.
The resulting CSD is still in the ambient space
\tilde{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq M_{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
and it is invariant along the fiber
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
Then,
we apply the projection
\pi:\tilde{M}_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
to the
support, and we obtain the CSD constructed here.

Since we have already constructed a CSD with the ambient space
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{r}

in the previous section
directly
for any (possibly degenerate) fixed data,
we do not need to repeat the above construction of a CSD.
Thus, our strategy here is just borrowing
the faithful representation of
G

induced from the one of
\tilde{G}
,
and applying it to the wall elements when needed.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a seed for a given (possibly degenerate) fixed data
\Gamma
.
From now on, we also use the internal-sum notation for

\tilde{N}=N\oplus M^{\circ}
and
\tilde{M}^{\circ}=\mathrm{Hom}(\tilde{N}^{\circ},\mathbb{Z})=M^{\circ}\oplus N

such as
\tilde{n}=n+m
,
\tilde{m}=m+n
, (
n\in N
,
m\in M^{\circ}
).
This notation is compatible with the notation in
(
0.4.2
) because

\{(n,0),(n^{\prime},0)\}=\{n,n^{\prime}\}
.
Also, for
n\in N
and
\tilde{m}=m+n^{\prime}
(
m\in M^{\circ}
,
n^{\prime}\in N
),
we have

\displaystyle\langle n,\tilde{m}\rangle=\langle n,m\rangle.

(0.4.6)

We introduce a group homomorphism (cf. (
0.1.8
)).

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tilde{p}^{*}:&N&\rightarrow&\tilde{M}^{\circ}\\
&n&\mapsto&\{\cdot,n\}.\end{matrix}

(0.4.7)

By the definition of
\tilde{p}^{*}
and (
0.4.2
), we have

\displaystyle\langle n^{\prime}+m^{\prime},\tilde{p}^{*}(n)\rangle=\{n^{\prime},n\}+\langle n,m^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\quad(n^{\prime}\in N^{\circ},n\in N,m^{\prime}\in M).

(0.4.8)

Lemma 0.4.4
.

The map
\tilde{p}^{*}
is injective.
In particular,
\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{1}),\cdots,\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{r})\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent.

Proof.

We take a basis

\mathfrak{s}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})

of
N

and a basis

(f_{1},\dots,\allowbreak f_{r},\allowbreak e_{1},\dots,e_{r})

of
\tilde{M}^{\circ}
.
Then, as (
0.1.11
),
we can easily verify that

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{ij}f_{i}+e_{j}.

(0.4.9)

Thus, the matrix representation of
\tilde{p}^{*}
is given by a
2r\times r
matrix

\displaystyle\tilde{B}=\begin{pmatrix}B\\
I\end{pmatrix},

(0.4.10)

where
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{r}
,
b_{ij}=\{\delta_{i}e_{i},e_{j}\}
as before.
The matrix
\tilde{B}
has rank
r
.
∎

Remark 0.4.5
.

The matrix
\tilde{B}
is the principal extension
of an exchange matrix
B
in
[
FZ07
]
.

In place of
P
in (
0.1.44
),
we define a monoid
\tilde{P}\subset\tilde{M}^{\circ}
as follows:

(i).

\tilde{P}=\sigma\cap\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
where
\sigma
is a
2r
-dimensional strongly convex cone in
\tilde{M}_{\mathbb{R}}=\tilde{M}^{\circ}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}
.

(ii).

\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{1}),\cdots,\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{r})\in\tilde{P}
.

Such
\tilde{P}
is not unique at all, and we choose one arbitrarily.
The results we will present here do not depend on the choice of
\tilde{P}
.
For example, we may take the monoid generated by

f_{1}
, …,
f_{r}
,
\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{1})
, …,
\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{r})
,
which are a basis of
\tilde{M}^{\circ}
by (
0.4.9
).

Lemma 0.4.6
.

For any
n\in N
with
n\neq 0
, there is some
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}
such that

\langle n,\tilde{m}\rangle\neq 0
.

Proof.

By the condition (i),
if
\langle n,\tilde{m}\rangle=0
for any
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}
,

\langle n,\tilde{m}\rangle=0
for any
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}

by linearity.
This is a contradiction.
∎

Let
\mathbbm{k}[\tilde{P}]
be the monoid algebra of
\tilde{P}
over
\mathbbm{k}
.
Let
J
be the maximal ideals of
\mathbbm{k}[\tilde{P}]

generated by
\tilde{P}\setminus\{0\}
,
and let

\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]

be the completion by
J
.
We express an arbitrary element
f\in\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]

as a formal power series in
x
as

\displaystyle f=\sum_{\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}}c_{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{m}}.

(0.4.11)

In parallel to (
0.1.46
),
we define the action of
X_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
(
n\in N^{+}
) on
\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{n}(x^{\tilde{m}}):=

\displaystyle\ \langle n,m\rangle x^{\tilde{m}+\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\quad(\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}).

(0.4.12)

Here and in the related formulas below,

\tilde{m}=m+n^{\prime}
(
m\in M^{\circ}
,
n^{\prime}\in N
).

Proposition 0.4.7
.

We have an injective group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tilde{\rho}_{x}:&G&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]])\\
&\exp(X)&\mapsto&\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}).\end{matrix}

(0.4.13)

Proof.

We only need to prove the injectivity,
since the rest is the same with Propositions
0.1.8

and
0.1.9
.
To show the injectivity, it is enough to prove the linear independence
of
\tilde{X}_{n}
(
n\in N^{+}
) in (
0.4.12
).
This follows from the injectivity of
\tilde{p}^{*}
and Lemma

0.4.6
.
∎

The representation
\tilde{\rho}_{x}
is
closely related with the mutations of
x
-variables
with principal coefficients
in
(
0.4.5
).
Thus, we call it the
principal
x
-representation
of
G
.

0.4.3
Faithful representation of wall elements

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be any seed for
a given (possibly degenerate) fixed data.

Lemma 0.4.8

(
[
GHKK18
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

For any
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
,
let

\displaystyle g=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}c_{j}X_{jn}\Biggr)\in G_{n}^{\parallel}

(0.4.14)

be an arbitrary element in
G_{n}^{\parallel}
.
Correspondingly, let

\displaystyle f_{g}:=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}jc_{j}x^{j\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\Biggr)\in\mathbbm{k}[[x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}]]\subset\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]].

(0.4.15)

Then, under the principal
x
-representation
\tilde{\rho}_{x}
,
g
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]
by

\displaystyle g(x^{\tilde{m}})=x^{\tilde{m}}f_{g}^{\langle n,m\rangle}\quad(\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}).

(0.4.16)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}g(x^{\tilde{m}})&=x^{\tilde{m}}\exp\Biggl(\sum_{j>0}c_{j}x^{j\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\langle jn,m\rangle\Biggr)\\
&=x^{\tilde{m}}\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}jc_{j}x^{j\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\Biggr)^{\langle n,m\rangle}=x^{\tilde{m}}f_{g}^{\langle n,m\rangle}.\end{split}

(0.4.17)

∎

Thanks to the injectivity of
\tilde{p}^{*}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{x}
,

g\in G
is uniquely specified by
f_{g}
and its action
(
0.4.16
).
Also, if we consider the normalized form of
g
in (
0.1.71
),
then
f_{g_{0}}
corresponding to
g_{0}=g^{1/\delta(n)}
is
also invariant under the rescaling of
\Gamma
.
In view of this,
we introduce the following notion.

Definition 0.4.9

(Normalized automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
)
.

For any
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
and

\displaystyle f=1+\sum_{j>0}c_{j}x^{j\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}\in\mathbbm{k}[[x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}]]\subset\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]],

(0.4.18)

we define an algebra automorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
of
\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]

by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}(x^{\tilde{m}}):=x^{\tilde{m}}f^{\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle}\quad(\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P})

(0.4.19)

We call
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
the
normalized automorphism
by
(n,f)
.

Definition 0.4.10

(Wall function)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{n}
(
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
) be any wall for
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
g_{0}:=g^{1/\delta(n)}
,
and let
f=f_{g_{0}}

be the formal power series
in
x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(n)}
in Lemma
0.4.8

corresponding to
g_{0}
.
Then,
g_{\mathfrak{d}}
acts on
\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]
by
\mathfrak{p}_{n,f}
.
Thus, we can specify the wall
\mathbf{w}
also by
\mathbf{w}=[\mathfrak{d},f]_{n}

without ambiguity,
where we use the square bracket to avoid confusion
with the usual notation.
We call
f
the
wall function
of a wall
\mathbf{w}
.

The above specification of a wall is used in
[
GHKK18
]
.

The following example is particularly important.

Example 0.4.11

(Cf. Proposition
0.1.12
)
.

For any
n\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
and
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
let
g=\Psi[tn]^{\delta(tn)}=\Psi[tn]^{\delta(n)/t}
,
where

\Psi[tn]
is the dilogarithm element
in (
0.1.50
).
Let

\displaystyle g_{0}:=g^{1/\delta(n)}=\Psi[tn]^{1/t}=\exp\Biggl(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}X_{jtn}\Biggr).

(0.4.20)

Then, the corresponding wall function
f=f_{g_{0}}
is given by

f[tn]
, where

\displaystyle f[tn]:=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}x^{j\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)}\Biggr)=1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)}.

(0.4.21)

Note that the factor
t^{-1}
in (
0.4.20
) cancels the factor
t
from
X_{jtn}

in (
0.4.21
).
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Psi[tn]^{\delta(tn)}(x^{\tilde{m}})=\mathfrak{p}_{n,f[tn]}=x^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)})^{\langle{\delta(n)}n,m\rangle}.

(0.4.22)

This automorphism is identified with the automorphism part of the Fock-Goncharov decomposition of mutations
of
x
-variables with principal coefficients in cluster algebras

[
FG09
, §2.1]
.
In terms of
f[tn]
,
the set of incoming walls in (
0.3.55
) is represented as

\displaystyle\mathrm{In}_{\mathfrak{s}}=

\displaystyle\ \{\mathbf{w}_{e_{i}}=[e_{i}^{\perp},1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{i})}]_{e_{i}}\mid i=1,\dots,r\}.

(0.4.23)

This is the description of a CSD given in
[
GHKK18
]
.

Notes

All results in this section are taken from

[
GHKK18
, §1, Appendix B]
and
[
GHK15
, §2]
,
and adapted for the present context.

0.5
Positive realization of CSD
and pentagon relation

In this section we
present an alternative construction of CSDs
following
[
GHKK18
]
.
The exponents of wall elements for the
resulting CSDs are all positive;
thus we call them
positive realizations
of CSDs.
This realization is a key to prove the Laurent positivity
of the corresponding cluster algebra.
The construction also reveals the fundamental role
of the pentagon relation for CSDs.

0.5.1
Positive realization of CSD

We start with a general fact on a consistent scattering diagram,
which easily follows from
Proposition
0.1.13
and the proof of Theorem
0.2.20
.
Recall the correspondence between
\Psi[tn]^{\delta(tn)}
and
f[tn]
in Example
0.4.11
.

Proposition 0.5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any consistent scattering diagram for a seed
\mathfrak{s}
.
Then, there is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}

that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}

such that
the wall element/function of any wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{n}=[\mathfrak{d},f]_{n}

of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}

has the following form

\displaystyle g=\Psi[tn]^{s\delta(tn)},\quad f=(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)})^{s}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\ s\in\mathbbm{k}).

(0.5.1)

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}(g)
be a consistent scattering diagram corresponding to
g\in G
.
We construct
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
by slightly modifying
the construction of
\mathfrak{D}(g)
in the proof of the surjectivity of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in Theorem
0.2.20
.
As in the proof of Proposition
0.1.13
, we uniquely expand
g_{0}^{z}
in (
0.2.48
) as

\displaystyle g_{0}^{z}=\prod_{j>0}\Psi[jn]^{c^{\prime}_{z,jn}}\quad(c^{\prime}_{z,jn}\in\mathbbm{k}).

(0.5.2)

Accordingly, we place
g_{\mathfrak{d}}
in (
0.2.49
) with

\displaystyle g_{\mathfrak{d}}=\begin{cases}\Psi[jn]^{c^{\prime}_{z,jn}}&\text{$\ell=j\deg(n)$ for some unique integer $j>0$},\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(0.5.3)

Then, the resulting consistent scattering diagram has
the property (
0.5.1
).
∎

The goal of this section is to prove the following
positivity result
on a CSD,
where the fact
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is crucial.

Theorem 0.5.2

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.13]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be a CSD for a seed
\mathfrak{s}
.
Then, there is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

such that
the wall element/function of any wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{n}=[\mathfrak{d},f]_{n}

of
\mathfrak{D}

has the following form

\displaystyle g=\Psi[tn]^{s\delta(tn)},\quad f=(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)})^{s}\quad(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(0.5.4)

In particular, the wall function
f
is a polynomial in
x
with positive coefficients.

We call the above CSD a
positive realization
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
By decomposing the wall in (
0.5.4
) into
s
walls with
g=\Psi[tn]^{\delta(tn)}
, we may assume that
s=1
for any wall whenever necessary.

Example 0.5.3
.

One can directly confirm the property (
0.5.4
) for finite and affine types of rank 2
by the results presented in Sections
0.2.2
and
0.3.5
,
respectively.
For walls other than (
0.3.69
) and (
0.3.81
),
we have
s=1
.
For the wall
(
0.3.69
)
, one can split it into infinitely many walls
so that the wall elements are given by

\displaystyle g=\Psi[2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{2\delta(2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0})}\quad(j=0,1,2,\dots).

(0.5.5)

Thus, only the case
s=2
in (
0.5.4
) appears.
Similarly, for the wall (
0.3.81
),
we have

\displaystyle g=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{3\delta(\mathbf{n}_{0})},\ \Psi[2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{2\delta(2^{j}\mathbf{n}_{0})}\quad(j=1,2,\dots).

(0.5.6)

Thus, the cases
s=2
and
3
in (
0.5.4
) appear.

0.5.2
Ordering Algorithm

Here we concentrate on a fixed data
\Gamma
of rank 2.
The following result on dilogarithm elements
is a key to prove Theorem
0.5.2
.
Let us
recall the notion of ordered and anti-ordered products
in Section
0.3.5
.
Namely, a (possibly infinite) product of

\Psi[n]^{c}
’s
(n\in N^{+},\ c\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0})
is

ordered
(resp.
anti-ordered
),
if, for any adjacent pair
\Psi[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}\Psi[n]^{c}
,

\{n^{\prime},n\}\leq 0
(resp.
\{n^{\prime},n\}\geq 0
) holds.

Proposition 0.5.4

(Ordering Lemma)
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be a seed for a fixed data
\Gamma
of rank 2.
Let

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}=\Psi[t^{\prime}_{j}n^{\prime}_{j}]^{s^{\prime}_{j}\delta(t^{\prime}_{j}n^{\prime}_{j})}\cdots\Psi[t^{\prime}_{1}n^{\prime}_{1}]^{s^{\prime}_{1}\delta(t^{\prime}_{1}n^{\prime}_{1})}\quad(n^{\prime}_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s^{\prime}_{i},t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

(0.5.7)

be any finite anti-ordered product.
Then,
C^{\mathrm{in}}

equals to a (possibly infinite) ordered product
C^{\mathrm{out}}
of
factors of the same form

\displaystyle\Psi[t_{i}n_{i}]^{s_{i}\delta(t_{i}n_{i})}\quad(n_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(0.5.8)

Moreover,
after gathering all powers of a common
\Psi[t_{i}n_{i}]
,
the above product is unique up to
the reordering of the commuting adjacent pairs with
n_{i}=n_{i+1}
.

When
\Gamma
is degenerate, the claim holds trivially by Proposition
0.1.14
(a).
Thus, we may concentrate on the case where
\Gamma
is nondegenerate.

The uniqueness is easily shown by a routine argument with Lemma
0.3.6
.
Namely, let
\pi_{\ell}(C^{\mathrm{out}})\in G^{\leq\ell}
, which is also regarded as an ordered product.
The uniqueness of the ordered product
\pi_{1}(C^{\mathrm{out}})
holds by Lemma
0.3.6
(b).
Suppose that the uniqueness holds for the ordered product
\pi_{\ell}(C^{\mathrm{out}})
.
Then, the uniqueness of the ordered product
\pi_{\ell+1}(C^{\mathrm{out}})

follows from the assumption and Lemma
0.3.6
(a).

To prove the existance, we introduce an algorithm based on the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
),
which is a systematic generalization of the one used in Section
0.3.5
.
In the algorithm,
without loss of generality, we may assume that
\{e_{2},e_{1}\}=1

as before.
Let us write
\Psi[n]
as
[n]
, for simplicity, as in Section
0.3.5
.

Definition 0.5.5

(
p
-exchangeable pair)
.

We say that, for any integer
p>0
, an anti-ordered adjacent pair
[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}[n]^{c}
is
p
-exchangeable

if
\{n^{\prime},n\}=p
and
c^{\prime}=j^{\prime}/p
and
c=j/p
for some
j^{\prime},j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Note that we can apply the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) for such a pair
by decomposing them into factors
[n^{\prime}]^{1/p}
and
[n]^{1/p}
.

Example 0.5.6
.

Let
[t_{1}n_{1}]^{s_{1}/t_{1}}[t_{2}n_{2}]^{s_{2}/t_{2}}

(n_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

be an anti-ordered adjacent pair with
\{t_{1}n_{1},t_{2}n_{2}\}=p
.
Then,
p=t_{1}t_{2}\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
is a multiple of both
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Thus, it is
p
-exchangable.

Here is the algorithm with annotation.

Algorithm 0.5.7

(Ordering Algorithm)
.

Fix any degree
\ell>0
.
For any finite anti-ordered product
C^{\mathrm{in}}

with factors of the form
[tn]^{s/t}

(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
,
obtain a finite ordered product
C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell})
of
factors of the form

[tn]^{s/t}

(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

by the following procedure.
(The fact
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
is obvious from the algorithm,
but the fact
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is not so,
and it will be shown in the proof of Lemma

0.5.10
.)

(
p_{0}
).

This is the main routine. Below
p_{0}:=1
.

1.

Set
C_{0}=C^{\mathrm{in}}
. (initial data)

2.

Decompose every factor
[n]^{c}

(c\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

in
C_{0}
into a product of a single factor
[n]
.
(Here we distinguish a single factor
[n]^{c}
and a product
[n]\cdots[n]
of a single factor
[n]
.)

3.

Repeat the following operation to
C_{0}
in this order until we do nothing.

i.

Pick up any adjacent pair
[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}[n]^{c}
in
C_{0}
.
(
c^{\prime}
and
c
are 1 or non-integers.)

ii.

If
\{n^{\prime},n\}<0
(ordered), we do nothing.

iii.

If
\{n^{\prime},n\}=0
(parallel),

a.

If
\deg(n^{\prime})>\deg(n)
, we exchange it as
[n]^{c}[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}
.
(Align parallel vectors from left to right in the increasing order of degree.)

b.

Otherwise, we do nothing.

iv.

If
\{n^{\prime},n\}>0
(anti-ordered),

a.

If
\deg(n^{\prime}+n)>\ell
, we exchange it as
[n]^{c}[n^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}
.
(They are commutative modulo
G^{>\ell}
.)

b.

If
\{n^{\prime},n\}=p_{0}
and
c^{\prime}=c=1
(
p_{0}
-exchangeable),
replace it with

[n][n^{\prime}+n][n^{\prime}]
.
(Apply the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=p_{0}
.)

c.

Otherwise, we do nothing.

4.

If the resulting
C_{0}
is ordered,
we join all factors
[n]^{c_{1}},\dots,[n]^{c_{j}}
with common
n

(which are now adjacent to each other) into a single factor

[n]^{c_{1}+\cdots+c_{j}}
.
Then, we set
C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell}):=C_{0}
,
and the process completes.

5.

Otherwise,
pick up any
p_{1}
-exchangeable adjacent pair in
C_{0}
,

\displaystyle C_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{1}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}[n_{1}]^{c},\quad\{n^{\prime}_{1},n_{1}\}=p_{1}\quad(c^{\prime},c\in(1/p_{1})\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

(0.5.9)

for some integer
p_{1}>p_{0}
, and go to the subroutine (
p_{1}
).

(
p_{1}
).

1.

Set
C_{1}=C_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
.

2.

Decompose every factor
[n]^{c}

(c\in(1/p_{1})\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

in
C_{1}
into a product of a single factor
[n]_{p_{1}}:=[n]^{1/p_{1}}
.

3.

Repeat the same process (3) of (
p_{0}
) for
C_{1}
,
where

*

[n]
is replaced with
[n]_{p_{1}}
.

*

p_{0}
is replaced with
p_{1}
.

(In particular, the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
) with
c=p_{1}

is applied to
p_{1}
-exchangeable anti-ordered adjacent pairs
in
C_{1}
.)

4.

If the resulting
C_{1}
is ordered,
we join all factors
[n]^{c_{1}}_{p_{1}},\dots,[n]^{c_{j}}_{p_{1}}
with common
n

into a single factor
[n]^{(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{j})/{p_{1}}}
.
(The case
c_{i}\neq 1
may happen by the result of the forthcoming subroutine (
p_{2}
).)
Then, we set
C_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}:=C_{1}
,
replace
C_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
in
C_{0}
with
C_{1}^{\mathrm{out}}
, and we come back to
the process (2) of (
p_{0}
).
(The “compatibility” of the exponent
(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{j})/p_{1}
in the parental routine (
p_{0}
) is the issue we are going to examine.)

5.

Otherwise,
pick up any
p_{2}
-exchangeable adjacent pair

\displaystyle C_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{2}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}_{p_{1}}[n_{2}]^{c}_{p_{1}}\quad\{n^{\prime}_{2},n_{2}\}=p_{2}\quad(c^{\prime},c\in(p_{1}/p_{2})\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

(0.5.10)

in
C_{1}

for some integer
p_{2}\neq p_{1}
, and go to the subroutine (
p_{2}
).
(Since
n_{2}^{\prime}
and
n_{2}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of
n_{1}^{\prime}
and
n_{1}
,

p_{2}>p_{1}
and
p_{2}
is a multiple of
p_{1}
.)

(
p_{2}
).

1–3.

Repeat the same process (1)–(3) of
(p_{1})

by replacing
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
.

4.

If the resulting
C_{2}
is ordered,
join all factors
[n]^{c_{1}}_{p_{2}},\dots,[n]^{c_{j}}_{p_{2}}
with common
n

into a factor

[n]_{p_{1}}^{(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{j})p_{1}/{p_{2}}}
.
Then, we set
C_{2}^{\mathrm{out}}:=C_{2}
,
replace
C_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}
in
C_{1}
with
C_{2}^{\mathrm{out}}
, and
we come back to the process (2) of (
p_{1}
).
(Again, the “compatibility” of the exponent
(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{j})p_{1}/{p_{2}}
in the parental routine (
p_{1}
) is the issue.)

5.

Otherwise, go to the next subroutine (
p_{3}
) for some
p_{3}>p_{2}
.

See Figure
7
for a schematic diagram of
the flow of the algorithm.
Note that each sequence of subroutines
p_{0}=1<p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots
has a finite depth,
because
p_{i}\leq\ell^{2}
.

We say that the above algorithm
fails
if, at some stage,

C_{i}
is not ordered and all remaining anti-ordered adjacent pair
therein is not
p
-exchangeable for any integer
p>0
.
As mentioned in Algorithm
0.5.7
,
some fractional power created by a subroutine
may cause the failure of the process.
For example, in (
0.3.73
),
if the power of
[3,2]
is
1/2
, instead of
1
therein,
one cannot carry out the remaining ordering in (
0.3.74
),
because the pentagon relation with
c=1
is not applicable.
We are going to show in Lemma

0.5.10

that such a situation never occurs.

If the algorithm does not fail, then
the algorithm completes (successfully) in finite steps
by the following reason:

•

Due to the structure of the pentagon relation (
0.1.56
),
all factors created in the subroutine (
p_{i}
) are
of higher degree than the factors of the initial
p_{i}
-exchangeable adjacent pair.

•

In particular, for the lowest degree
\ell_{0}
of
the factors in
C^{\rm{in}}
,
there is no new factor of degree
\ell_{0}
that is
created during the ordering.

•

Then, by the induction on
\ell^{\prime}
with
\ell_{0}\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
,
we can show that
the number of the factors of degree
\ell^{\prime}

that are created during the ordering has some upper bound.

C^{\mathrm{in}}
C^{\mathrm{out}}(\ell)
p_{0}=1
p_{1}
p_{2}
p^{\prime}_{1}
p^{\prime}_{2}
\ell^{2}
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 7:
Flow of Ordering Algorithm.

Let us go back to the situation in Proposition
0.5.4
.
For a given
C^{\mathrm{in}}
therein, suppose that the algorithm does not fail for any
\ell
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}\equiv C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell})\mod G^{>\ell},\quad\pi_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}(C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell^{\prime}}))=C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell}).

(0.5.11)

Let
C^{\mathrm{out}}:=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}C^{\mathrm{out}}({\ell})
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}=C^{\mathrm{out}},

(0.5.12)

and
C^{\mathrm{out}}
is a (possibly infinite) ordered product of
factors

\displaystyle\Psi[t_{i}n_{i}]^{s_{i}\delta(t_{i}n_{i})}\quad(n_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\ s_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}),

(0.5.13)

where we may assume that all
\Psi[t_{i}n_{i}]
are mutually distinct
by joining the factors with common
\Psi[t_{i}n_{i}]
.
The positivity of
s_{i}
follows from the fact that the pentagon relation
does not involve negative factors.

To prove that
s_{i}
is an integer,
we use the following property of the principal
x
-representation
\tilde{\rho}_{x}
.

Lemma 0.5.8
.

Under the principal
x
-representation
\tilde{\rho}_{x}
,

\Psi[tn]^{s\delta(tn)}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}[[\tilde{P}]]
if and only if

s
is an integer.

Proof.

Recall (see Example
0.4.11
) that the action of

\Psi[tn]^{s\delta(tn)}
(
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s\in\mathbb{Q},\ t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

on
\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]

is given by

\displaystyle\Psi[tn]^{s\delta(tn)}(x^{\tilde{m}})=x^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)})^{s\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle}\quad(\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}).

(0.5.14)

Thus,
the if-part is clear.
Suppose that
s
is not an integer.
Note that
the coefficient of the term
x^{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(tn)}

in (
0.5.14
)
is
c=s\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle
.
Then, it is enough to show that
c
is not an integer for some
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}
.
Let
a_{0}>0
be the smallest positive integer such that

\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle\in a_{0}\mathbb{Z}
for any
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}
.
By the condition (i) of the definition of
\tilde{P}
in
Section
0.4.2
,

\tilde{P}
contains a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
for example
\tilde{m}+f_{i},\tilde{m}+e_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,r
)
for some
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}
.
Then, by
\mathbb{Z}
-linearity,
we have
\langle\delta(n)n,m\rangle\in a_{0}\mathbb{Z}
for any
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
.
If
a_{0}\neq 1
,
we have
\delta(n)n/a_{0}\in N^{\circ}
,
which contradicts the definition of
\delta(n)
.
Thus,
a_{0}=1
. Then,
c=s
is not an integer for
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{P}

attaining the minimum
a_{0}=1
.
∎

Lemma 0.5.9
.

The factor
s_{i}
in (
0.5.13
) is an integer.

Proof.

By Lemma
0.5.8
, the product
C^{\mathrm{in}}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}[[\tilde{P}]]
.
Then, again by Lemma
0.5.8
,
we can prove that
all
s_{i}
in (
0.5.13
) are integers
by the induction on
\deg(t_{i}n_{i})
.
∎

It remains to show that Algorithm
0.5.7
never fails.
This is proved by the same method for Lemma
0.5.9
.

Lemma 0.5.10
.

For any
C^{\mathrm{in}}
,
Algorithm
0.5.7
never fails,
so that it completes in finitely many steps.

Proof.

We first note a key observation to prove the lemma.

For the first subroutine (
p_{1}
) entered from the main
routine
(p_{0})
,
the following facts hold:

(
i
).

The initial product
C^{\mathrm{in}}_{1}
in (
0.5.9
) has the form

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}_{1}=[t_{1}n_{1}]^{s_{1}/t_{1}}[t_{2}n_{2}]^{s_{2}/t_{2}}\quad(n_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(0.5.15)

(
o
).

If the algorithm does not fail,
any factor in the final product
C^{\mathrm{out}}_{1}
also has the form

[tn]^{s/t}

with the same condition in (
0.5.15
).

The property (
o
) follows from the property (
i
) by applying the same proof of
Lemma
0.5.9
, where we set
N^{\circ}=N
,
M^{\circ}=M

and consider the action on
\mathbb{Z}[[\tilde{P}]]
.

Similarly, but slightly differently, suppose that a subroutine
(p_{i})

(i\geq 1)
starts with
the initial product

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}=[\tilde{n}_{1}]^{s_{1}}_{p_{i}}[\tilde{n}_{2}]^{s_{2}}_{p_{i}},\quad\{\tilde{n}_{1},\tilde{n}_{2}\}=p_{i}\quad(\tilde{n}_{i}\in N^{+},\ s_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(0.5.16)

Let

\displaystyle N_{p_{i}}^{+}:=\{a_{1}\tilde{n}_{1}+a_{2}\tilde{n}_{2}\in N^{+}\mid a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ a_{1}+a_{2}>0\}.

(0.5.17)

Let
(N_{p_{i}}^{+})_{\mathrm{pr}}
be the set of all primitive vectors in
N_{p_{i}}^{+}

with respect to
N_{p_{i}}^{+}
(not with respect to
N^{+}
).
Note that, for any
n_{1},n_{2}\in N_{p_{i}}^{+}
,

\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
is a multiple of
p_{i}
.
Then, for a subroutine (
p_{i+1}
)
(i\geq 1)
entered from a
subroutine
(p_{i})
,
the following facts hold:

(
i
).

The initial product
C^{\mathrm{in}}_{i+1}
has the form

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}_{i+1}=[t_{1}n_{1}]^{s_{1}/t_{1}}_{p_{i}}[t_{2}n_{2}]^{s_{2}/t_{2}}_{p_{i}}\quad(n_{i}\in(N_{p_{i}}^{+})_{\mathrm{pr}},\ s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(0.5.18)

(
o
).

If the algorithm does not fail,
any factor in the final product
C^{\mathrm{out}}_{i+1}
also has the form

[tn]^{s/t}_{p_{i}}

with the same condition in (
0.5.18
).

Again, the property (
o
) follows from the property (
i
) by
the same argument as above,
where we consider the action on
(1/p_{i})\mathbb{Z}[[\tilde{P}]]
,
and
N
and
M^{\circ}
for
\tilde{P}
are replaced with
the sublattice
N_{p_{i}}
generated by
\tilde{n}_{1}
and
\tilde{n}_{2}

and its dual lattice
M_{p_{i}}
, respectively.

Let us prove the lemma together with the property (
i
)
in both cases
along the flow of the algorithm.

1.

The algorithm starts from the main routine (
p_{0}
),
where the initial product
C^{\mathrm{in}}
certainly has factors of the
form
in (
0.5.15
) by assumption.
Also,
any factor created by the pentagon relation from a
1
-exchangeable pair has the form
[tn]^{s}

with
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
; in particular,
it has the form in (
0.5.15
).
Suppose that we are in the process (5) of the routine (
p_{0}
).
Take any remaining anti-ordered pair
[t_{1}n_{1}]^{s_{1}/t_{1}}[t_{2}n_{2}]^{s_{2}/t_{2}}
,
and set
p_{1}:=\{t_{1}n_{1},t_{2}n_{2}\}
.
Then, by Example
0.5.6
,
it is
p_{1}
-exchangeable.
Thus, we go to the subroutine (
p_{1}
) with
C_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfying the property (
i
).

2.

In the subroutine
(p_{1})

we decompose
C_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
into integer powers of
[n]_{p_{1}}
,
and after ordering all
p_{1}
-exchangeable pairs by the pentagon relation,
we only obtain integer powers of
[n]_{p_{1}}
.
Thus, if we have an anti-ordered pair in the process (5) of the routine (
p_{1}
),
it has the form in (
0.5.18
) with
\{t_{1}n_{1},t_{2}n_{2}\}=p_{2}>p_{1}

and
s_{i}/t_{i}
are integers.
In particular,
the pair is
p_{2}
-exchangeable, and
we go to the next subroutine (
p_{2}
) with
C_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfying the property (
i
).
(So far, we do not encounter a serious problem.)

3.

The process continues
until we get to the deepest subroutine
(p_{k})
.
Then, by definition,
(p_{k})
returns the ordered product
C^{\mathrm{out}}_{k}

satisfying the property (
o
) to
the subroutine
(p_{k-1})
.
We claim that the subroutine
(p_{k-1})
continues to run.
(The following is the key argument.)
In fact, suppose that we still have any anti-ordered pair
in
C_{k-1}
. Then,
it has the form

\displaystyle[t_{1}n_{1}]_{p_{k-1}}^{s_{1}/t_{1}}[t_{2}n_{2}]_{p_{k-1}}^{s_{2}/t_{2}}\quad(n_{i}\in(N_{p_{k-1}}^{+})_{\mathrm{pr}},\ s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

(0.5.19)

thanks to the property (
o
) just we had.
Let
p^{\prime}_{k}=\{t_{1}n_{1},t_{2}n_{2}\}>0
.
Since
\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
is a multiple of
p_{k-1}
as already mentioned,

p^{\prime}_{k}
is a multiple of both
t_{1}p_{k-1}
and
t_{2}p_{k-1}
.
Thus, the pair is
p^{\prime}_{k}
-exchangeable.
If
p^{\prime}_{k}=p_{k-1}
, we stay in the subroutine
(p_{k-1})
.
If
p^{\prime}_{k}>p_{k-1}
, we go to another subroutine
(
p^{\prime}_{k}
) again with
C_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}
satisfying the property (
i
).
The same story continues.

Thus, the algorithm never fails.
∎

By Lemmas
0.5.9
and
0.5.10
,
we obtain Proposition
0.5.4
.
Moreover, as a special case of Proposition
0.5.4
,
we obtain Theorem
0.5.2
in the rank 2 case.

Corollary 0.5.11
.

Theorem
0.5.2

holds for
\mathrm{rank}\,N=2
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
0.5.4
to
the product
C^{\mathrm{in}}=\Psi[e_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}\Psi[e_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}
.
∎

Example 0.5.12
.

It is not difficult to translate Algorithm
0.5.7

into a computer program.
The source code is given in Appendix

.8

for the reader’s convenience.

of non-affine infinite type
calculated by computer.

,

Let us examine the behavior
in the Badlands
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})

defined in (
0.3.88
).
The condition (
0.3.89
) for a normal vector
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})
is given as follows:

\displaystyle(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle=(1,5),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5+\sqrt{5}}=0.276\dots

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\leq\frac{2}{5-\sqrt{5}}=0.723\dots,

(0.5.25)

\displaystyle(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle=(1,6),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3+\sqrt{3}}=0.211\dots

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{3-\sqrt{3}}=0.788\dots,

(0.5.26)

\displaystyle(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle=(2,3),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3+\sqrt{3}}=0.422\dots

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\leq\frac{2}{3-\sqrt{3}}=1.577\dots,

(0.5.27)

\displaystyle(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle=(2,4),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2+\sqrt{2}}=0.292\dots

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2-\sqrt{2}}=1.707\dots,

(0.5.28)

\displaystyle(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})

\displaystyle=(3,3),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3+\sqrt{5}}=0.381\dots

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}\leq\frac{2}{3-\sqrt{5}}=2.618\dots.

(0.5.29)

The corresponding elements
\Psi[n]
are inside the
parentheses in (
0.5.20
)–(
0.5.24
).
We observe that, in each case,
every

n\in N^{+}
with
\deg(n)\leq 7
satisfying the above condition
appears in the product.
This agrees with (and even stronger than) the expectation of
[
GHKK18
]

explained in Section
0.3.5
.
We expect this is true up to any degree, and we do not have any counterexample so far.

Let us also present an example of the ordering of a more general
anti-ordered product in the left hand side.
We have, modulo
G^{>9}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\,\begin{bmatrix}0\\

4\end{bmatrix}^{1/2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2/3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\\
&\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\

3\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
