belongs to
\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}
, which is abelian as in Lemma
0.3.6
.
Then, by repeating the same argument as
\ell=1
,
we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\ell+1}})=\pi_{\ell+1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\overline{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}_{\ell+1}})\pi_{\ell+1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\ell+1}}^{-1})=\mathrm{id}.

(0.6.52)

Then, again by the decomposition of
\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell+1,\ell}
,
which is parallel to Lemma
0.3.6
,
we conclude that
(
0.6.49
) holds for
\ell+1
.
∎

0.6.4
Proof of mutation invariance of CSDs

Let us complete the proof of Theorem
0.6.9
.
We have already seen in Lemma
0.6.16
that

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is an
extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)
.

We first prove the consistency of

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
as an
extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)
.
Let us introduce the following algebra automorphism
of
R_{k}=R^{\prime}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tilde{S}_{k}=\tilde{S}_{k,\mathfrak{s}}:&R_{k}&\rightarrow&R_{k}\\
&x^{\tilde{m}}&\mapsto&x^{\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})}=x^{\tilde{m}+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}\quad(\tilde{m}\in\overline{P}),\\
&(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-1}&\mapsto&(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-1}.\end{matrix}

(0.6.53)

Lemma 0.6.18
.

The following equalities hold as an algebra automorphism of
R_{k}
:

\displaystyle\Psi[S^{*}_{k}(n)]\circ\tilde{S}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{S}_{k}\circ\Psi[n]\quad(n\in{N}^{+}),

(0.6.54)

\displaystyle\Psi[e^{\prime}_{k}]^{\delta_{k}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{S}_{k}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{-\delta_{k}}.

(0.6.55)

Proof.

Here, we consider the action only on
x^{\tilde{m}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\Psi[S^{*}_{k}(n)]\circ\tilde{S}_{k})(x^{\tilde{m}})&=\Psi[S^{*}_{k}(n)](x^{\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})})\\
&=x^{\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})}\tilde{S}_{k}(f[n])^{\langle S^{*}_{k}(n),{\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})}\rangle}\\
&=\tilde{S}_{k}(x^{\tilde{m}}f[n]^{\langle n,m\rangle})\\
&=(\tilde{S}_{k}\circ\Psi[n])(x^{\tilde{m}}),\end{split}

(0.6.56)

where we used Lemma
0.6.6
(a) and (d).
Also, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi[e^{\prime}_{k}]^{\delta_{k}}(x^{\tilde{m}})&=x^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e^{\prime}_{k})})^{\langle\delta_{k}e^{\prime}_{k},m\rangle}\\
&=x^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{-\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle}\\
&=x^{\tilde{m}+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle}\\
&=\tilde{S}_{k}(x^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle})\\
&=(\tilde{S}_{k}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{-\delta_{k}})(x^{\tilde{m}}).\end{split}

(0.6.57)

The action on
(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{-1}
is similar and easier.
∎

Lemma 0.6.19
.

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is a consistent
extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)
.

e_{k}^{\perp}
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
z_{0}
\gamma
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 12:
Admissible loop
\gamma
in the proof of Lemma

0.6.19
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the consistency
as an extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)
.
In view of Lemma
0.2.14
,
it is enough to show that
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})}=\mathrm{id}
for any admissible loop
\gamma
.
If
\gamma
is entirely in
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
, the claim follows from the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

as an extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s},k)

in Lemma
0.6.16
(a).
If
\gamma
is entirely in
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}
, the claim follows from the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

and (
0.6.54
).
Therefore, it is enough to consider the case where
\gamma
crosses
the hyperplane
e_{k}^{\perp}
as in Figure
12
,
where
z_{0}
is the base point of
\gamma
.
Then, from the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}=g_{+}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{-\delta_{k}}\circ g_{-}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{\delta_{k}}=\mathrm{id},

(0.6.58)

where
g_{\pm}
is the contribution from each half space
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\pm}
.
Then, by the definition of
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
and Lemma
0.6.18
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})}&=(\tilde{S}_{k}\circ g_{+}\circ\tilde{S}_{k}^{-1})\circ\Psi[e^{\prime}_{k}]^{\delta_{k}}\circ g_{-}\circ\Psi[e^{\prime}_{k}]^{-\delta_{k}}\\
&=\tilde{S}_{k}\circ(g_{+}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{-\delta_{k}}\circ g_{-}\circ\Psi[e_{k}]^{\delta_{k}})\circ\tilde{S}_{k}^{-1}\\
&=\mathrm{id}.\end{split}

(0.6.59)

∎

Next we prove the following property.

Lemma 0.6.20
.

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is equivalent to
an extended scattering diagram
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)

with

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathrm{in}}=\mathrm{In}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}},

(0.6.60)

where
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i}:=\mathbf{w}_{e^{\prime}_{i}}
for simplicity.
For the sake of convenience, we restate here what should be proved.

Claim.

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is equivalent to
an extended scattering diagram
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)

satisfying the following properties:

(i).

\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
contains a unique wall
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k}=(e^{\prime}_{k}{}^{\perp},\Psi[e^{\prime}_{k}]^{\delta_{k}})_{e^{\prime}_{k}}
.

(ii).

For any
i\neq k
,

\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
contains a unique wall
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i}=(e^{\prime}_{i}{}^{\perp},\Psi[e^{\prime}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e^{\prime}_{i}}
.

(iii).

All other walls in
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
are outgoing.

The property (i) holds for
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
by the construction.
So, let us consider the properties (ii) and (iii).
We note that, by the construction of
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
in Definition
0.6.7
,
a wall
S_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{+})
is incoming if and only
if
\mathbf{w}_{+}
is incoming.
Thus, any incoming wall in
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})

other than
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{k}

only comes from
\mathbf{w}_{i,+}=((e_{i}{}^{\perp})_{+},\Psi[e_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e_{i}}
or
\mathbf{w}_{i,-}=((e_{i}{}^{\perp})_{-},\Psi[e_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e_{i}}

(i\neq k)
.
For each
i\neq k
, we consider three cases.

Case 1.

\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}>0
.
We have
p^{*}(e_{i})\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{w}_{i,+}
is an incoming wall.
Also,
S_{k}^{*}(e_{i})=e_{i}+\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}e_{k}=e^{\prime}_{i}
.
Therefore, we have an incoming wall in
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
,

\displaystyle S_{k}(\mathbf{w}_{i,+})=((e^{\prime}_{i}{}^{\perp})_{-},\Psi[e^{\prime}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i,-},

(0.6.61)

where the sign
\pm
of
(e^{\prime}_{i}{}^{\perp})_{\pm}
is with respect to
e^{\prime}_{k}=-e_{k}
.
Then, up to equivalence, it is extended to

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i}
by adding outgoing walls.

Case 2.

\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}<0
.
We have
p^{*}(e_{i})\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{w}_{i,-}
is an incoming wall.
Also,
e_{i}=e^{\prime}_{i}
.
Therefore, we have an incoming wall in
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i,-}=((e^{\prime}_{i}{}^{\perp})_{+},\Psi[e^{\prime}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{e^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i,+}.

(0.6.62)

Again, up to equivalence, it is extended to

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i}
by adding outgoing walls.

Case 3.

\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}=0
.
We have
p^{*}(e_{i})\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{w}_{i,+}

and
\mathbf{w}_{i,-}
are
both incoming walls, where
p^{*}(e_{i})
is at the boundary of both walls

\mathbf{w}_{i,\pm}
.
Also,
S_{k}^{*}(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{i}
.
Therefore, we have incoming walls
\mathbf{w}_{i,+}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i,-}
and
\mathbf{w}_{i,-}=\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i,+}

in
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Then, joining them back again, we have

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{i}
.

It is clear that the resulting extended scattering diagram
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}

satisfies all desired properties.
∎

By
Lemmas
0.6.19
and
0.6.20
,
up to equivalence,

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
satisfies the assumption
of
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
in Proposition
0.6.17
(by replacing
\mathfrak{s}
therein with
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
).
Thus, we conclude that

T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}

as an extended scattering diagram for
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},k)
.
Moreover,
since our choice of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is with minimal support by Proposition
0.5.18
,
so is
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}(T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}))=\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}).

(0.6.63)

This implies that
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is actually
an ordinary scattering diagram for
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
,
and that it is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}

as an ordinary scattering diagram for
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
0.6.9
.

0.6.5
Admissible region

In view of the remark after Theorem
0.6.9
,
we have the following corollary of Theorem
0.6.9
.

Corollary 0.6.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be a CSD for
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
n=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}e_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}

such that
n\neq e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
,
and let
z
be any general point in
n^{\perp}
.
For each
k=1,\dots,r
, we define

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{k}=\begin{cases}-a_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}[-\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{j}\}]_{+}a_{j}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+},\\
-a_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}[\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{j}\}]_{+}a_{j}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}.\end{cases}

(0.6.64)

Then, we have

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})\neq\mathrm{id}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{$b^{\prime}_{k}\geq 0$ for any $k$.}

(0.6.65)

It is an interesting problem to give a direct proof of this property
and also to extend it to other classes of consistent scattering diagrams.

Let us call the region of
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
that satisfies
the condition
b^{\prime}_{k}\geq 0
for any
k
the
admissible region
for a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
where nontrivial outgoing walls are allowed.

Figure 13:
Admissible region for a CSD of rank 2.

Example 0.6.22
.

Let us explicitly write down the condition (
0.6.65
)
for the rank 2 CSDs in Sections
0.2.2
and
0.3.5
.
For
\{e_{2},e_{1}\}=0
, no
n\neq e_{1},e_{2}
is allowed.
This agrees with the CSD of type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
therein.
For
\{e_{2},e_{1}\}=1
,
the quantities in (
0.6.64
) are given as

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{1}=\begin{cases}-a_{1}+\delta_{1}a_{2}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{+},\\
-a_{1}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{-}.\end{cases}\quad b^{\prime}_{2}=\begin{cases}-a_{2}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{2}^{+},\\
-a_{2}+\delta_{2}a_{1}&z\in\mathcal{H}_{2}^{-},\end{cases}

(0.6.66)

where
a_{1},a_{2}\neq 0
.
Then, the condition
b^{\prime}_{k}\geq 0
(
k=1,2
) is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle z\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{+}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}^{-},\quad\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}\leq\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}}\leq\delta_{2}.

(0.6.67)

The first condition is equivalent to that a wall is outgoing,
and the second condition follows from the formula
(
0.1.75
).
Therefore, we directly verified
the property (
0.6.65
) in the rank 2 case.
Let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the point corresponding to
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then, by (
0.2.12
),
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{1}^{-1}a_{1}z_{1}+\delta_{2}^{-1}a_{2}z_{2}=0.

(0.6.68)

Thus, the condition (
0.6.67
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle z_{1}\geq 0,\quad z_{2}\leq 0,\quad-\delta_{2}\leq\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}\leq-\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}.

(0.6.69)

The admissible region is depicted in Figure
13
.

0.6.6
Chamber structure,
G
-fan, and cluster complex

There is an important consequence of the mutation invariance of CSDs.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is a CSD with minimal support.
We first give few remarks:

(a).

By Lemma
0.2.18
,
the interior
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}})
of the positive orthant

\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is a
chamber
of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
,

namely, it is a connected component
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Also,
since
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is an open set,
its boundary belongs to
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

(b).

For
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})
,
we define a cone in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
of full dimension by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}:=T_{k,\mathfrak{s}}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}).

(0.6.70)

Since
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}})

is a chamber of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}})
,

\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}})
is a
chamber of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})

by (
0.6.30
).
More explicitly,
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
is the cone
defined by,
for
i=k
,

\displaystyle\langle e^{\prime}_{k},T_{k}(z)\rangle=\langle-e_{k},z+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},z\rangle p^{*}(e_{k})\rangle=-\langle e_{k},z\rangle\geq 0,

(0.6.71)

and,
for
i\neq k
, by (
0.6.71
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle e^{\prime}_{i},T_{k}(z)\rangle&=\langle e_{i}+[\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}]_{+}e_{k},z+[\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},z\rangle]_{+}p^{*}(e_{k})\rangle\\
&=\langle e_{i},z\rangle+[\{\delta_{k}e_{k},e_{i}\}]_{+}\langle e_{k},z\rangle\geq 0.\end{split}

(0.6.72)

Thus, the cone
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
is adjacent to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+}

with a common boundary in
e_{k}^{\perp}
.

(c).

Let
\sigma
be a face of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}

of codimension 1
with the normal vector
n
.
Let
z\in\sigma
be general.
Then,
g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
is obtained by
the pull-back of
g_{T_{k}(z)}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}})=\Psi[e^{\prime}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}}

by
T_{k}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}.

(0.6.73)

Moreover,
\delta(n)=\delta_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
.

We repeat the above argument for a sequence of mutations of seeds.

Definition 0.6.23

(
G
-cone/
G
-chamber)
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
is a CSD with minimal support.
We consider any sequence of mutations of seeds

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mu_{k_{0}}}}\mathfrak{s}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mu_{k_{2}}}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mu_{k_{i-1}}}}\mathfrak{s}_{i}.

(0.6.74)

We define
a cone in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}:=(T_{k_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ T_{k_{i-1},\mathfrak{s}_{i-1}}^{-1})(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}).

(0.6.75)

As a special case
i=0
, we set
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Then,

\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}})
is
a chamber of
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
We call
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
and
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}})
a
G
-cone
and a
G
-chamber
of

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
(In
[
GHKK18
]
,
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
is called a
cluster chamber
.)

Let
\mathbb{T}_{r}
be the
r
-regular tree
wth edges labelled by
1
, …,
r

such that all edges emanating from each vertex
t
of
\mathbb{T}_{r}
have different labels.
We abuse the notation
\mathbb{T}_{r}
for the set of all vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{r}
.
We fix the initial vertex
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{r}
arbitrarily.
Then, we attach
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}
to
t_{0}
.
Also, we attach
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
in (
0.6.75
)
to
t_{i}
, where
t_{0}
and
t_{i}
are sequentially connected with edges
labelled by
k_{0}
, …,
k_{i-1}
.
Thus, the
G
-cones of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
are parametrized by
\mathbb{T}_{r}
,
though this parametrization is not necessarily one-to-one.
Then,
under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
f_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
,
any
G
-cone
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
is identified with the cone
\sigma(G^{t_{0}}_{t_{i}})
spanned by the column vectors (the
g
-vectors
) of the
G
-matrix

G^{t_{0}}_{t_{i}}

in
[
FZ07
]

for the corresponding cluster pattern.
The collection of
G
-cones
\sigma(G^{t_{0}}_{t})
(
t\in\mathbb{T}_{r}
) and their faces constitutes a fan

\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
(the
G
-fan
).
Moreover, as a simplicial complex, the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})
is isomorphic to the
cluster complex

in
[
FZ03a
]
.
See Part II for details.

By repeatedly applying the result (
0.6.73
), we have the following property.

Proposition 0.6.24
.

Let
\sigma
be a face of a
G
-cone
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}

of codimension 1
with the normal vector
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
.
Then, for any general
z\in\sigma
, we have

\displaystyle g_{z}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})=\Psi[n]^{\delta(n)}.

(0.6.76)

Moreover,
\delta(n)=\delta_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
.

The above result implies that,
in Theorem
0.5.2
,
for the walls that are faces of
G
-cones,
only
s=t=1
occurs.

Example 0.6.25
.

(a).
Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be any CSD of finite type
with rank 2 or 3
in Sections
0.2.2
and
0.3.5
.
Then, the
G
-cones fill up the entire ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
In other words,

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})

is naturally identified with the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})

of the corresponding cluster pattern.
In particular,
the property (
0.6.76
) holds
for any wall of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
.

(b).
Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be the CSD of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

in Section
0.3.5
.
Then, the
G
-cones fill up the set
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1)
.
In other words,

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})\setminus\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1)

in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1)

is naturally identified with the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})

of the corresponding cluster pattern.
In particular,
the property (
0.6.76
) holds
for any wall in
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})\setminus\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1)
.
On the other hand,
the wall in (
0.3.69
) is
unreachable

by
cluster mutations
(i.e., mutations in the sense of cluster algebras).
The
A_{2}^{(2)}
case is similar.
For the non-affine type of rank 2 in Section
0.3.5
, replace
\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1)

with the Badlands
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})\setminus\{0\}
therein,
which is also unreachable by cluster
mutations.

0.6.7
Rank 3 examples: infinite type

Let us continue to give examples of rank 3 CSDs
based on Construction
0.5.14
.
Here we consider the CSDs of
infinite type
,
which are further separated into
affine
and
non-affine
types.
We use the same convention in Section
0.5.4

to present rank 3 CSDs.

Figure 14:
CSD of type
A_{2}^{(1)}
.
The thick arc is unreachable by cluster
mutations.

(a). Type
A_{2}^{(1)}
. As a prototypical example of affine type, we consider the following case

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&-1\\
1&0&-1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=\delta_{3}=1.

(0.6.77)

As a new feature compared with the finite type,
we have perpendicular joints of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
in Section

0.3.5
,
which are given by

\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1}\subset(1,1,0)^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\perp}
and

\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2}\subset(0,1,1)^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp}
in Figure
14
.
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{e}_{3},(1,1,0)\}=\{(0,1,1),\mathbf{e}_{1}\}=2.

(0.6.78)

We put this number 2
in the figure in the position of incoming direction,
so that outgoing walls appear in the opposite direction.
These joints
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2}
are connected by walls with the common normal vector
(1,1,1)
,
and their wall elements are given by

\Psi[(1,1,1)]^{2}
,
\Psi[(2,2,2)]
,
\Psi[(4,4,4)]^{1/2}
, …,
based on the result
the CSD of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

in Section

0.3.5
.
The union of their supports is the cone
\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})
,
and it
is represented by a thick arc in Figure
14
.
It corresponds to the unreachable wall of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
with the normal vector
(1,1)

in Example
0.5.14
(b).
Note that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2}

are directly connected only by
\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})
, because
a hyperplane
containing
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

is unique.
Consequently,
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2}
“alternately” create new walls,
and they pile up as shown in the figure.
We see that those walls with normal vectors

\displaystyle(2,1,1),\ (2,2,1),\ (3,2,2),\ (3,3,2),\ \dots

(0.6.79)

converge to the thick arc
from above
in the figure.
Similarly,
those walls with normal vectors

\displaystyle(1,1,2),\ (1,2,2),\ (2,2,3),\ (3,2,2),\ \dots

(0.6.80)

converges to the thick arc
from below
.
This agrees with the well-known results on
the cluster patterns of type
A_{2}^{(1)}
by
[
FZ02
]

and
[
CI12
]
.
We conclude that the
G
-cones fill up the region
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus(\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})\setminus\{0\})
.
In other words,

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})\setminus(\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})\setminus\{0\})

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus(\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})\setminus\{0\})

is naturally identified with the
G
-fan
\Delta(\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}})

of the corresponding cluster pattern.
The walls in
\sigma(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}_{2})
is
unreachable
by cluster
mutations.

Figure 15:
Example of rank 3 CSD of non-affine type.
The close-up of the shaded region is given in Figure
16
.

(b). Non-affine type. As a prototypical example of non-affine type, we consider the following case

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&-2\\
0&2&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=\delta_{3}=1.

(0.6.81)

The CSD has the remarkable
fractal
nature.
This
nature is simply originated in the fractal structure of the underlying

r
-regular tree
\mathbb{T}_{r}
(
r\geq 3
) for the corresponding cluster complex,
and it has been observed in some figures in the literature (e.g.,
[
FG16
,
NC13
]
)
for the so-called Markov quiver.
This implies that it is impossible to draw a precise picture in principle.
Thus, we change the strategy as follows:

•

We set the goal to obtain a reasonably clear picture of the
Badlands of rank 3
.

•

We abandon to faithfully follow Construction
0.5.14

by uniformly increasing the degree
\delta(n)
of a newly attached wall element
\Psi[n]
.
Rather, we do the mixture of Construction
0.5.14
and “cluster mutation approach”.
Namely,
having Construction
0.5.14
in mind,
we increase the region of
G
-cones in the scattering diagram.
Since
the Badlands happens to be the complement of the
G
-fan in this example,
the shape of the Badlands will gradually emerge by this procedure.

•

While proceeding, we carefully choose added walls
so that both local and global structures of the Badlands are better visible.
(For example, we ignored some wall with degree 12,
while we added a new wall of degree 245.)

•

Due to the complexity we double-checked the result
with a computer program for computing
c
-vectors

of the cluster pattern.
More specifically, we used SageMath 9.4
with
Cluster Algebras package
by D. Rupel and S. Stella.

The resulting CSD is depicted in Figures
15
and
16
.

Figure 16:
Uncompleted picture of the Badlands of rank 3.
This is the close-up of the shaded region in Figure
15

Figures
15
gives the big picture of the CSD.
Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{>0}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}^{>0}\cap\mathcal{H}_{3}^{<0}
,
which corresponds to the shaded region in Figure
15
.
Outside the region
\mathcal{R}
, the structure of the CSD is clearly presented in the figure,
where there are actually infinitely many walls converging to the
unreachable walls attached to joints of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

represented by two thick arcs.
There is no more additional wall outside
\mathcal{R}
.

The close-up of the region
\mathcal{R}
is given in Figure
16
.
This is where the Badlands and its fractal nature appear.
Here are remarks and observations:

•

A three-digit number such as 032 represents the normal vector (0,3,2)
of a wall crossing the boundary of the region
\mathcal{R}
.

•

A number such as 4 at a joint in
n_{1}^{\perp}\cap n_{2}^{\perp}

represents
|\{n_{1},n_{2}\}|=4
.
It is put in the position of incoming direction,
so that outgoing walls appear in the opposite direction
as in the previous case.

•

A thick arc represents the supports of walls attached to a joint of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

that is unreachable by cluster mutations.
They consist of the “backbone” of the total shaded region (explained below).
Six thick arcs are presented here, but in the limit
\ell\rightarrow\infty
there will be
infinitely many ones added in the top and bottom parts of the back-born.
However, they will be so small and tightly aligned that they are not visible in this page size anyway.

•

An unshaded triangle is already completed under the algorithm,
and it is a
G
-cone.
Meanwhile, a shaded triangle or quadrilateral is not completed yet.
Thus, when the degree
\ell
increases,
the corners are sharpened and new corners appear.
In other words,
the total shaded region reduces,
and the complexity of the boundary increases.

•

By the above comments and the picture presented in Figure
16
,
it is already clear that in the limit
\ell\rightarrow\infty

the total shaded region (in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) converges to some connected closed domain, say
\mathcal{B}
.
It is also characterized as the unreachable region by cluster mutations.
The boundary of
\mathcal{B}
is a fractal.
Moreover,
if the density conjecture on the Badlands of rank 2 in Section

0.3.5
holds,
the set
\mathcal{B}
is also densely filled with nontrivial walls.
Thus, we call
\mathcal{B}
the
Badlands
of this CSD.

Some other examples of rank 3 CSD of infinite type are found in
[
Mul16
,
Rea
]
.
It seems that there are several patterns for non-affine infinite type CSDs even for the rank 3 case,
and to classify them is an interesting problem.

Notes

Most results are taken from
[
GHKK18
, §1.3, Appendix C.2]

with added/modified proofs.
In particular,
the proof of Lemma
0.6.16

is significantly simplified from
the one of
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.28]

by
based on Construction
0.5.14

and Proposition
0.1.24
(b).

0.7
Broken lines and theta functions

In this section we introduce broken lines and theta functions for scattering diagrams.
We prove the positivity of coefficients of theta functions for cluster scattering diagrams.

0.7.1
Broken lines and theta functions

In this section we again use the principal
x
-representation for
wall elements.
For notational simplicity, for any
\tilde{m}_{i}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}=M^{\circ}\oplus N
,
let
m_{i}
denote the
M^{\circ}
-component of
\tilde{m}_{i}
.
Namely,
\tilde{m}_{i}=m_{i}+n_{i}
(
m_{i}\in M^{\circ}
,
n_{i}\in N
) for some
n_{i}
.
We recall that
\langle n,\tilde{m}_{i}\rangle=\langle n,m_{i}\rangle
holds
for any
n\in N
by (
0.4.6
).

Definition 0.7.1

(Broken line)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any scattering diagram for a given initial seed
\mathfrak{s}
.
Let
\tilde{m}_{0}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}\setminus\{0\}
and
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.
A
broken line for
\tilde{m}_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is a piecewise-linear
curve
\gamma:(-\infty,0]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
satisfying the following properties:

(1).

The endpoint
\gamma(0)
is
Q
.

(2).

The initial velocity
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
in
t\rightarrow-\infty

is
-m_{0}
.

(3).

The curve
\gamma
bends
and change velocity only when it intersects walls of
\mathfrak{D}
.
(It can cross walls also
without bending and changing velocity
.)
Moreover,
when
\gamma
bends, it always
crosses
walls,
not
reflects
at them.

More conditions follow below.
Suppose that
\gamma
bends
J\geq 0
times.
Let
t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{J}<0
be the points (“time”) of the parameter
t

for
\gamma(t)
when
\gamma
bends.
(It may cross multiple walls with a common normal vector at each time
t_{j}
.)
We set
t_{0}=-\infty
and
t_{J+1}=0
.
Thus,
\gamma

is linear and with constant velocity in each interval
I_{j}=(t_{j},t_{j+1})
(
j=0,\dots,J
).
As an extreme case, for
J=0
,
the curve
\gamma
is a half line ending at
Q

with constant velocity
-m_{0}
.
To each interval
I_{j}
,
we attach a monomial
c_{j}x^{\tilde{m}_{j}}\in\mathbbm{k}[\tilde{M}^{\circ}]
recursively as follows:

(4).

To the initial interval
I_{0}
, the monomial
x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}
is attached,
where
\tilde{m}_{0}
is the given data.

(5).

For
j\geq 1
, let
\gamma_{j}
be a segment of
\gamma
for
the interval

(t_{j}-\delta,t_{j}+\delta)
with sufficiently small
\delta>0
such that
\gamma_{j}
crosses walls
only at
t_{j}
.
Then,
c_{j}x^{\tilde{m}_{j}}
is a monomial in

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{j},\mathfrak{D}}(c_{j-1}x^{\tilde{m}_{j-1}})
,
where
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{j},\mathfrak{D}}
acts as the principal
x
-representation.
See (
0.7.5
) for more explicit description.

Finally, the velocity of
\gamma
in the interval
I_{j}
is given as follows:

(6).

The velocity
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
in the interval
I_{j}
is given by
-m_{j}
,
where
c_{j}x^{\tilde{m}_{j}}
is the attached monomial.
This agrees with the conditions (2) and (4).

This completes the definition of a broken line.
We then attach a monomial in
\mathbbm{k}[\tilde{M}^{\circ}]
to each broken line
\gamma
by

\displaystyle c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}_{\gamma}}:=c_{J}x^{\tilde{m}_{J}}.

(0.7.1)

Several remarks are in order.

(a).
At
t_{j}
, the velocity changes
from
-m_{j}
to
-m_{j+1}=-m_{j}-k_{j}p^{*}(n_{j})
, where
n_{j}
is the common normal vector
of the walls at
t_{j}
, and
k_{j}
is some positive integer.
Since
p^{*}(n_{j})\in n_{j}^{\perp}
,
the broken line always
crosses
walls,

not reflects nor stops
at them as stated in the condition (3).

(b). In particular, we have
\tilde{m}_{j}=\tilde{m}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{i})

(a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
.

(c).
For
\tilde{m}^{\prime}=\tilde{m}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{i})
,
we define the degree
\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}^{\prime})
by

\displaystyle\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}^{\prime}):=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}.

(0.7.2)

Then, by (a), we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}_{0})=0<\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}_{1})<\cdots<\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}_{J})=\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}_{\gamma}).

(0.7.3)

(d).
For the segment
\gamma_{j}
in the condition (5),
suppose that
\gamma_{j}
crosses walls

[\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},f_{{\lambda}}]_{n_{j}}

(\lambda\in\Lambda_{j}
)
at
t_{j}
.
Note that
\gamma^{\prime}=-m_{j-1}
just before
\gamma
crosses these walls.
Then,
the intersection signs
in (
0.2.6
)
are given by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{j}=\begin{cases}1&\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle>0,\\
-1&\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle<0.\\
\end{cases}

(0.7.4)

Thus,
\epsilon_{j}\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle=|\langle n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle|
.
Therefore, by Definition
0.2.6
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{j},\mathfrak{D}}(c_{j-1}x^{\tilde{m}_{j-1}})=c_{j-1}x^{\tilde{m}_{j-1}}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{j}}f_{{\lambda}}^{|\langle\delta(n_{j})n_{j},m_{j-1}\rangle|}.

(0.7.5)

It is crucial that there is
no division
by
f_{{\lambda}}
in this expression
for the forthcoming positivity of theta functions.

Definition 0.7.2

(General position)
.

We say that
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
is
in general position
if
for each
\ell>0
there is some neighborhood
U_{\ell}
of
Q

such that, for the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
, any broken line for
Q^{\prime}\in U_{\ell}

converges to a broken line for
Q

in the limit
Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q
.
(Namely, no broken line crosses
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})
in the limit.)

Below we always and implicitly assume that
Q
is in general position.

Definition 0.7.3

(Theta function)
.

Under the same assumption and notations in Definition

0.7.1
,
the
theta function
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}
for
\tilde{m}_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is defined by

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}:=\sum_{\gamma\in B(Q,\tilde{m}_{0})}c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}_{\gamma}},

(0.7.6)

where
B(Q,\tilde{m}_{0})
is the set of all broken lines for
\tilde{m}_{0}
with endpoint
Q
.
We also set

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,0}=1.

(0.7.7)

The function
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}
only depends on the equivalence class
of
\mathfrak{D}
, though the condition for
Q
to be in general position
depends on the choice of a representative of
\mathfrak{D}
.

Lemma 0.7.4

(
[
GHKK18
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}\in x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]].

(0.7.8)

Proof.

By Remark (b),
each term
c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}_{\gamma}}
belongs to
x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]
.
Thus, it is enough to show that,
for a given
\tilde{m}^{\prime}\in\tilde{m}_{0}+\tilde{P}
,
there are only finitely many
\gamma
such that

\tilde{m}_{\gamma}=\tilde{m}^{\prime}
.
We note the following facts:

•

By the constraint (
0.7.3
),
such
\gamma
bends at most
\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}^{\prime})
times.

•

The walls of
\mathfrak{D}
that contribute to
\tilde{m}^{\prime}

belongs to the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
at
\ell=\deg_{\tilde{m}_{0}}(\tilde{m}^{\prime})
,
which has only finitely many walls.

•

At each bending there are only finitely many
possibilities of bending.

Therefore, there are only finitely many possibilities of such
\gamma
.
∎

Proposition 0.7.5

(
[
GHKK18
, Proposition 3.8]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any scattering diagram for a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
,
and let

\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be the positive orthant
in (
0.2.40
).
For
\tilde{m}_{0}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
suppose that
m_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}\cap M^{\circ}
.
Also, suppose that
Q\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}=x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}.

(0.7.9)

Proof.

We may assume that
\tilde{m}_{0}\neq 0
.
It is clear that

Q+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{0}

is a broken line for
\tilde{m}_{0}
with end point
Q
,
and it does not intersect any walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
;
therefore, the associated monomial is
x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}
.
We claim that under the condition there is no broken line
\gamma

with bending.
Suppose that such
\gamma
exists.
We use the notation in Definition
0.7.1
.
For each break point
t_{j}
therein,
let
n_{j}\in N^{+}
be a unique (not necessarily primitive)
element such that

\gamma
intersects walls in
n_{j}^{\perp}
at
t_{j}

and that the velocity of
\gamma
is shifted by
-p^{*}(n_{j})
.
Let
L_{j}
be the segment of
\gamma
for the interval
I_{j}
.
Then, we claim the following:

\displaystyle L_{j}\subset H_{j}^{-}:=\biggl\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\biggm|\biggl\langle\sum_{i=1}^{j}n_{i},z\biggr\rangle\leq 0\biggr\}\quad(j=1,\dots,J).

(0.7.10)

We show it by the induction on
j
. This is true for
j=1
, because the support of the wall crossed by
\gamma
at
t_{1}

is in
n_{1}^{\perp}
, and
\gamma
is in
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}}
for
t\rightarrow-\infty
.
Now suppose that (
0.7.10
) hold up to
j
.
Then, we have
\langle\sum_{i=1}^{j}n_{i},\gamma(t_{j+1})\rangle\leq 0
.
Also,
\langle n_{j+1},\gamma(t_{j+1})\rangle=0
.
Thus,
\gamma(t_{j+1})\in H_{j+1}^{-}
.
Moreover, the new velocity vector
\gamma^{\prime}_{j+1}=-m_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}p^{*}(n_{i})

satisfies the inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}\biggl\langle\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}n_{i},\gamma^{\prime}_{j+1}\biggr\rangle&=\biggl\langle\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}n_{i},-m_{0}\biggr\rangle+\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}n_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}n_{i}\biggr\}\\
&=\biggl\langle\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}n_{i},-m_{0}\biggr\rangle\leq 0.\end{split}

(0.7.11)

Thus, the fact (
0.7.10
) holds for
j+1
.
Since
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}})\cap H_{j}^{-}=\emptyset
,
\gamma
cannot reach
Q\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
∎

0.7.2
Transitivity on theta functions

Now we assume that
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent.

Proposition 0.7.6

(
[
CPS
, Lemma 4.9]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram.
Let
\tilde{m}_{0}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
and
Q,\,Q^{\prime}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.
Then, for any admissible curve
\gamma
from
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},\tilde{m}_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}).

(0.7.12)

The rest of this subsection is devoted to
prove this proposition.
We may assume that
\tilde{m}_{0}\neq 0
.

Let
J
be the maximal ideal of

\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]
generated by
\tilde{P}\setminus\{0\}
.

Lemma 0.7.7

(
[
CPS
, Lemma 4.7]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
be the reduction of
\mathfrak{D}
at
\ell
.
Suppose that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are in the same chamber of

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})
.
Then, we have the equality

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}\equiv\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},\tilde{m}_{0}}\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.13)

Proof.

Since we consider
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}

only modulo

x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}
,
one can replace
\mathfrak{D}
with

\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
to calculate
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}
.
First,
suppose that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are connected by a curve
\beta

in the same chamber
such that any point on
\beta
is in general position for
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
.
Then, each broken line with endpoint
Q

continuously deformed to a broken line with endpoint
Q^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the theta function is unchanged.

Suppose that there is no such curve between
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
.
Let us consider the special case where
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are connected by a curve
\beta

in the same chamber
such that there is a unique point
Q_{0}
on
\beta

that is not in general position.
(The general case reduces to this case.)
Then, there is at least one broken line
\gamma
with endpoint
Q

such that, in the limit
Q\rightarrow Q_{0}
,
the broken line intersects with some boundary

\partial\mathfrak{d}
or joint
\mathfrak{j}=\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
.

(a)
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
\gamma_{A}
\gamma_{B}
\mathfrak{j}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
(b)
\gamma
\gamma^{\prime}
\gamma_{A}
\gamma_{B}
\mathfrak{c}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 17:
Broken lines in the proof of Lemma

0.7.7
.

First, let us consider the case
\mathfrak{j}
.
See Figure
17
(a).
Suppose that
\gamma
crosses the walls
that contain the joint
\mathfrak{j}

with the incoming attached monomial
c_{\mathrm{in}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{in}}}
and the outgoing attached monomial

c_{\mathrm{out}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{out}}}
.
There may be several broken lines
\gamma_{1}=\gamma
, …,
\gamma_{p}

with endpoint
Q

such that
their bending patterns differ from
\gamma
only
inside these walls.
They have the common
incoming attached monomial
c_{\mathrm{in}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{in}}}

and the common exponent
\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{out}}
for the outgoing attached monomials.
Then, all these broken lines have a common limit
for
Q\rightarrow Q_{0}
.
Let
\gamma_{A}
be the half of a small loop around
\mathfrak{j}
,
which is on the same side of
\mathfrak{j}
with
\gamma_{i}
’s
as in Figure
17
(a).
Then, the sum
of the outgoing attached monomials
for
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{p}

is exactly the terms of the monomials in
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{A},\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(c_{\mathrm{in}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{in}}})

with exponents
x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{out}}}
.
We repeat the same argument to the opposite side of
\mathfrak{j}

with the opposite half
\gamma_{B}
of
the small loop around
\mathfrak{j}
.
Then, by the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{A},\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(c_{\mathrm{in}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{in}}})\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{B},\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(c_{\mathrm{in}}x^{\tilde{m}_{\mathrm{in}}})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.14)

Thus, passing through
Q_{0}
does not change

\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}

modulo

x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}
.
Therefore, we obtain the equality (
0.7.13
).

Next, we consider the remaining case
\partial\mathfrak{d}
.
See Figure
17
(b).
We may concentrate on a face
\mathfrak{c}
of
\mathfrak{d}
of codimension 2
such that
\mathfrak{c}
is not a joint.
Then,
\mathfrak{c}
is contained in some walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
with the common normal vector
n
.
In this case, for each broken line for
Q
, there is a
unique broken line for
Q^{\prime}
with the same attached incoming and outgoing monomial
modulo
x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}
, thanks to the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}
.
Thus, passing through
Q_{0}
does not change

\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}

modulo

x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}
.
∎

Lemma 0.7.8

(
[
CPS
, Lemma 4.8]
)
.

Suppose that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are in different chambers of

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})
.
Then, for any admissible curve
\gamma
from
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
,
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},\tilde{m}_{0}}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.15)

Proof.

We may assume that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are in adjacent chambers of

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\ell})
,
since the general case follows from this case
by composing the automorphisms
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}
.
Let
n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+}
be the normal vector of the walls
separating
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
.
We decompose

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}=\sum_{\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}}c_{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{m}}=\vartheta_{+}+\vartheta_{0}+\vartheta_{-},

(0.7.16)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{+}=\sum_{\scriptstyle\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,m\rangle>0}c_{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{m}},\quad\vartheta_{0}=\sum_{\scriptstyle\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,m\rangle=0}c_{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{m}},\quad\vartheta_{-}=\sum_{\scriptstyle\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}\atop\scriptstyle\langle n,m\rangle<0}c_{\tilde{m}}x^{\tilde{m}}.

(0.7.17)

Also, we decompose
\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},\tilde{m}_{0}}=\vartheta^{\prime}_{+}+\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}+\vartheta^{\prime}_{-}

in the same way.
Thanks to Lemma
0.7.7
,
we may take
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
to be very close to the wall and also move parallel to the wall
inside each chamber as we like.
Without loosing generality, one can assume that
\langle n,Q\rangle>0
and
\langle n,Q^{\prime}\rangle<0
.

Q^{\prime}
Q
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 18:
Broken lines in the proof of Lemma

0.7.8
.

(i).
\vartheta_{+}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{+}
.
For any exponent
\tilde{m}
of
\vartheta^{\prime}_{+}
,

\langle n,m\rangle>0
holds.
Then,
any broken line for
\vartheta^{\prime}_{+}

is obtained by extending a broken line for
\vartheta_{+}
.
See Figure
18
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{+}^{\prime}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(\vartheta_{+})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.18)

(ii).
\vartheta_{-}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{-}
.
The situation is opposite, and we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{-}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}^{-1}(\vartheta^{\prime}_{-})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.19)

(iii).
\vartheta_{0}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}
.
The broken lines for both do not cross the walls separating
Q
and
Q^{\prime}

in the final step.
Then,
the proof of Lemma
0.7.7
applies
to
\vartheta_{0}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}
,
and
we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0}^{\prime}\equiv\vartheta_{0}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(\vartheta_{0})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}.

(0.7.20)

We can unify the above three equalities into a single one

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},\tilde{m}_{0}}\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}(\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}})\mod x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}J^{\ell+1}

(0.7.21)

as desired.
∎

Since
\ell
is arbitrary in (
0.7.15
), we have the desired equality (
0.7.12
).
This completes the proof of Proposition
0.7.6
.

0.7.3
Positivity and mutation invariance of theta functions

Now let us specialize to a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

for a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
.

We have the following fundamental result on the positivity of theta functions for a CSD.

Theorem 0.7.9

(Positivity of theta functions
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.13 & Remark 3.2]
)
.

For a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
every theta function
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}\in x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}\mathbbm{k}[[\tilde{P}]]

has only positive integer coefficients.

Proof.

We choose a positive realization of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

in Theorem
0.5.2
,
so that every wall function
f[tn]^{s}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
has
only positive integer coefficients.
Then,
the product in the right hand side
of (
0.7.5
) has also
only positive integer coefficients.
Thus,
c_{\gamma}
in (
0.7.1
) is a positive integer.
Therefore, the theta function defined by (
0.7.6
)
has
only positive integer coefficients.
∎

In the rest, let us present the mutation invariance
of theta functions of a CSD
and its consequences.
Let us define a piecewise-linear transformation in parallel to
T_{k}
in
(
0.6.25
),

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tilde{T}_{k}=\tilde{T}_{k,\mathfrak{s}}:&\tilde{M}^{\circ}&\rightarrow&\tilde{M}^{\circ}\\
&\tilde{m}&\mapsto&\tilde{m}+[\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle]_{+}\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})=\begin{cases}\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})&m\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+},\\
\tilde{m}&m\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-},\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(0.7.22)

where
\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m})
is the one in (
0.6.10
).

Proposition 0.7.10

(
[
GHKK18
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

For a given seed
\mathfrak{s}
, let
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{s})

be the mutation of
\mathfrak{s}
in direction
k
.

(a). There is a one-to-one correspondence between
the broken lines for
\tilde{m}_{0}
with endpoint
Q

with respect to a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

and
the broken lines for
\tilde{T}_{k}(\tilde{m}_{0})
with endpoint
T_{k}(Q)

with respect to a CSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}=T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.
The locus of the line is transformed by
the piecewise-linear map
T_{k}
in (
0.6.25
).

(b). (Mutation invariance of theta functions.)
The theta function
\vartheta^{\mathfrak{s}}_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

and the one

\vartheta^{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}

are related by

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}_{T_{k}(Q),\tilde{T}_{k}(\tilde{m}_{0})}=\begin{cases}\tilde{S}_{k}(\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}^{\mathfrak{s}})&Q\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+},\\
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}^{\mathfrak{s}}&Q\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}.\end{cases}

(0.7.23)

where
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\pm}
is the one
in (
0.6.23
),
and
\tilde{S}_{k}
acts on the exponents of
x
in
\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Proof.

(a).
In
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
the walls and broken lines are unchanged,
while
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}
the walls and broken lines are transformed by the linear map
S_{k}
.
Thus, the rule for broken lines are preserved in each
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\pm}
.
Therefore,
we only need to check the rule when a broken line crosses the wall
\mathbf{w}_{e_{k}}=[e_{k}^{\perp},1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}]_{e_{k}}
.

Case (1). Suppose that a broken line
\gamma
with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
crosses
e_{k}^{\perp}

from
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}
with attached monomial
cx^{\tilde{m}}
.
Then,
\langle e_{k},-m\rangle>0
.
After crossing
e_{k}^{\perp}
, the new attached monomial is a term
in
cx^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle|}
.
Applying
\tilde{S}_{k}
, it transforms to a term in

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ cx^{\tilde{m}+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle|}\\
&=cx^{\tilde{m}-|\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle|\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle|}\\
&=cx^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e^{\prime}_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e^{\prime}_{k},m\rangle|}.\end{split}

(0.7.24)

This is the rule for a broken line with respect to
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Case (2). Suppose that a broken line
\gamma

with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}

crosses
e_{k}^{\perp}

from
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+}
with attached monomial
cx^{\tilde{m}}
.
Then,
\langle e_{k},-m\rangle<0
.
After crossing
e_{k}^{\perp}
, the new attached monomial is a term
in
cx^{\tilde{m}}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle|}
,
which is rewritten as

\displaystyle cx^{\tilde{m}+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}(1+x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e^{\prime}_{k})})^{|\langle\delta_{k}e^{\prime}_{k},m\rangle|}.

(0.7.25)

Since the attached monomial for the incoming segment is transformed
from
cx^{\tilde{m}}

to
cx^{\tilde{m}+\langle\delta_{k}e_{k},m\rangle\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{k})}
,
this is the correct rule for a broken line with respect to
T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}})
.

(b).
Let
\gamma
be a broken line with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
,
and let
\gamma^{\prime}=T_{k}(\gamma)
be
the corresponding broken line with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}
.
Let
c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}_{\gamma}}

and
c^{\prime}_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{\gamma}}

be the monomials attached with

\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, respectively.
Then, by (a), we have

\displaystyle c^{\prime}_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}^{\prime}_{\gamma}}=\begin{cases}c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{S}_{k}(\tilde{m}_{\gamma})}&Q\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{+},\\
c_{\gamma}x^{\tilde{m}_{\gamma}}&Q\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{-}.\end{cases}

(0.7.26)

Thus, we have (
0.7.23
).
∎

We have the following corollary of
Propositions
0.7.5

and
0.7.10
.

Corollary 0.7.11

(
[
GHKK18
, Corollary 3.9]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be a CSD,
and let

\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
be
the
G
-cone
in (
0.6.75
).
For
\tilde{m}_{0}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
suppose that
m_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\cap M^{\circ}
.
Also, suppose that
Q\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}})
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}=x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}.

(0.7.27)

Proof.

By (
0.6.75
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}=(T_{k_{i-1},\mathfrak{s}_{i-1}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{k_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}})(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}).

(0.7.28)

Let
T:=T_{k_{i-1},\mathfrak{s}_{i-1}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{k_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}

and
\tilde{T}:=\tilde{T}_{k_{i-1},\mathfrak{s}_{i-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{T}_{k_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
.
Let
S:M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
be the linear map
that locally coincides with
T
on the cone
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
.
Let
\tilde{T}
and
\tilde{S}
be the corresponding maps for
\tilde{M}^{\circ}\rightarrow\tilde{M}^{\circ}
.
Then, applying
(
0.7.23
)
repeatedly, we obtain

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}_{T(Q),\tilde{T}(\tilde{m}_{0})}=\tilde{S}(\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}),

(0.7.29)

where
\tilde{S}
acts on the exponents of
x
.
Meanwhile,

\vartheta^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}_{T(Q),\tilde{T}(\tilde{m}_{0})}=x^{\tilde{T}(\tilde{m}_{0})}

by
Proposition
0.7.5
.
Thus, we have

\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}=x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}
.
∎

Combining Proposition
0.7.6
and Corollary
0.7.11
,
we have another fundamental theorem on theta functions for a CSD.

Theorem 0.7.12

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 4.9]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}
be a CSD,
and let

\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
be
the
G
-cone
in (
0.6.75
).
For
\tilde{m}_{0}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
,
suppose that
m_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\cap M^{\circ}
.
Also, suppose that
Q\in\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{+})
.
Then, for any admissible curve
\gamma
from any point in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}})

to
Q
, we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Q,\tilde{m}_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}}(x^{\tilde{m}_{0}}).

(0.7.30)

To conclude, let us describe
the relation between theta functions
and cluster variables (
x
-variables) for the corresponding cluster pattern with principal coefficients
in

[
FZ07
]
.
See Section 6 of Part II for a detailed account.

As explained briefly in Section
0.6.6
,
each

G
-cone
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
in (
0.6.75
)
is identified with a cone spanned by the
g
-vectors
\mathbf{g}_{1;t}
, …,
\mathbf{g}_{r;t}

of the
G
-matrix
G^{t_{0}}_{t}
for some
t\in\mathbb{T}_{r}
.
Let
\mathbf{x}_{t}=(x_{1;t},\dots,x_{r;t})
be the
cluster
at
t

for the corresponding cluster pattern with principal coefficients.
By the celebrated
Laurent phenomenon
by
[
FZ02
]
, any
cluster variables
x_{i;t}
(
i=1
, …,
r
)
are expressed as a Laurent polynomial in the initial cluster variables
\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})

and the initial principal coefficients
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
, where negative exponents appear only for variables
x_{i}
.
We identify
x^{\tilde{m}}
(
\tilde{m}\in\tilde{M}^{\circ}
) with the above variables
as
x^{f_{i}}=x_{i}
and
x^{e_{i}}=y_{i}
.
See (
0.1.7
).
We note that

\displaystyle x^{\tilde{p}^{*}(e_{i})}=y_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{r}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}

(0.7.31)

are the
\hat{y}
-variables

\hat{y}_{i}
in
[
FZ07
]
.
Under this identification,
it follows from Theorem
0.7.12

that the cluster variables
x_{i;t}
are identified with (a part of)
theta functions as

\displaystyle x_{i;t}=\vartheta_{Q,\mathbf{g}_{i;t}},

(0.7.32)

where the RHS is the one given in (
0.7.30
).

Notes

All results are taken from
[
GHKK18
, §3, §4]

and
[
CPS
, §4]

adapted to the present formulation of scattering diagrams.

.8
Source code of Ordering Algorithm

For the reader’s convenience we present a source code of Ordering Algorithm (Algorithm
0.5.7
)
written for SageMath 9.4
1
1

1

Sage Mathematics Software, The Sage Development Team,
https://www.sagemath.org.
.
It is an almost direct translation of Algorithm
0.5.7
,
and no effort is made for the efficiency and sophistication.
(If any minor or major bug is found, please let us know.)

The following examples explain
how to use it on Sage Notebook.

Example .8.1
.

(a).
Let us order the product
\Psi[e_{2}]^{5}\Psi[e_{1}]^{3}

modulo
G^{>5}
.

In:
order(5,[[0,1,5],[1,0,3]])
Out:
[[1, 0, 3],
[3, 1, 5],
[2, 1, 15],
[3, 2, 125],
[1, 1, 15],
[2, 2, 60],
[2, 3, 270],
[1, 2, 30],
[1, 3, 30],
[1, 4, 15],
[0, 1, 5]]

(b). One can order any (not necessarily anti-ordered) product
with factors of the form
\Psi[tn]^{s/t}

(n\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+},\ s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

as specified in Algorithm
0.5.7
.

In:
order(6,[[0,1,1],[1,2,1],[2,1,1],[2,2,1/2],[1,0,2]])
Out:
[[1, 0, 2],
[4, 1, 1],
[3, 1, 2],
[2, 1, 2],
[4, 2, 19],
[3, 2, 16],
[1, 1, 2],
[2, 2, 13/2],
[3, 3, 33],
[2, 3, 10],
[1, 2, 1],
[2, 4, 9/2],
[1, 3, 1],
[0, 1, 1]]

Enjoy exploring the Badlands!
